QCD at finite temperature and density within the fRG approach: An overview
Wei-jie Fu

Abstract
In this paper we present an overview on recent progress in studies of QCD at finite temperature and densities within the functional renormalization group (fRG) approach. The fRG is a nonperturbative continuum field approach, in which quantum, thermal and density fluctuations are integrated in successively with the evolution of the renormalization group (RG) scale. The fRG results for the QCD phase structure and the location of the critical end point (CEP), the QCD equation of state (EoS), the magnetic EoS, baryon number fluctuations confronted with recent experimental measurements, various critical exponents, spectral functions in the critical region, the dynamical critical exponent, etc., are presented. Recent estimates of the location of the CEP from first-principle QCD calculations within fRG and Dyson-Schwinger Equations, which passes through lattice benchmark tests at small baryon chemical potentials, converge in a rather small region at baryon chemical potentials of about 600 MeV. A region of inhomogeneous instability indicated by a negative wave function renormalization is found with μ B ≳ 420 \mu_{B}\gtrsim 420 MeV. It is found that the non-monotonic dependence of the kurtosis of the net-proton number distributions on the beam collision energy observed in experiments, could arise from the increasingly sharp crossover in the regime of low collision energy.



I
Introduction

One of the most challenging questions in heavy-ion physics arises from the attempt to understand how the deconfined quarks and gluons, i.e., the quark-gluon plasma (QGP), evolve into the confined hadrons. This evolution involves apparently two different phase transitions: One is the confinement-deconfinement phase transition and the other is the chiral phase transition related to the breaking and restoration of the chiral symmetry of QCD. When the strange quark is in its physical mass and the
u
and
d
quarks are massless, i.e., in the chiral limit, the chiral phase transition in the regime of small chemical potential in the QCD phase diagram, see e.g.,
Figure

14
, might be of second order, and belongs to the
O(4)
symmetry universality class
Pisarski and Wilczek 1984
;
Stephanov 2006a
. With the increase of the baryon chemical potential, the second-order phase transition might be changed into a first-order one at the tricritical point. When the
u
and
d
quarks are in their small, but nonvanishing physical masses, due to the explicit breaking of the chiral symmetry, the
O(4)
second-order chiral phase transition turns into a continuous crossover
Aoki et al. 2006
, which is also consistent with experimental measurements, cf. e.g.,
Andronic et al. 2018
. The tricritical point in the phase diagram evolves into a critical end point (CEP), which is the end point of the first-order phase transition line at high baryon chemical potential or densities.

Although the phase transition at the CEP is of second order and belongs to the
Z(2)
symmetry universality class, the location of CEP and the size of the critical region around the CEP are non-universal. From the paradigm of the QCD phase diagram described above, one can easily find that the CEP plays a pivotal role in understanding the whole QCD phase structure in terms of the temperature and the baryon chemical potential. As a consequence, it becomes an very important task to search for and pin down the location of the CEP in the QCD phase diagram. Lattice simulations provide us with a wealth of knowledge of QCD phase transitions at vanishing and small baryon chemical potential, cf. e.g.,
Bazavov et al. 2012
;
Borsanyi et al. 2013
;
Borsanyi et al. 2014
;
Bellwied et al. 2015
;
Bazavov et al. 2017a
;
Bazavov et al. 2017b
;
Borsanyi et al. 2018
;
Bazavov et al. 2019
;
Ding et al. 2019a
;
Ding et al. 2021
;
Bazavov et al. 2020
;
Borsanyi et al. 2020
, but due to the sign problem at finite chemical potentials, lattice calculations are usually restricted in the region of
\mu_{B}/T\lesssim 2\sim 3
, where no signal of CEP is observed
Karsch 2019
. Notably, recent estimates of the location of CEP from first-principle functional approaches, such as the functional renormalization group (fRG) and Dyson-Schwinger Equations (DSE), which passes through lattice benchmark tests at small baryon chemical potentials, converge in a rather small region at baryon chemical potentials of about 600 MeV
Fu et al. 2020a
;
Gao and Pawlowski 2021a
;
Gunkel and Fischer 2021
, and see also, e.g.,
Fischer 2019
for related discussions.

Search for the CEP is currently under way or planned at many facilities, see, e.g.,
Luo and Xu 2017
;
Bzdak et al. 2020
;
Andronic et al. 2018
;
Friman et al. 2011a
;
Agakishiev et al. 2009
;
Abgrall et al. 2014
;
Sorin et al. 2011
;
Blaschke et al. 2016
;
Dainese et al. 2019
;
Yang et al. 2013
;
Lü et al. 2017
;
Sakaguchi 2017
;
Sako 2019
. Since the CEP is of second order, the correlation length increases significantly in the critical region in the vicinity of a CEP. Moreover, it is well known that fluctuation observables, e.g., the fluctuations of conserved charges, are very sensitive to the critical dynamics, and the increased correlation length would result in the increase of the fluctuations as well. Therefore, it has been proposed that a non-monotonic dependence of the conserved charge fluctuations on the beam collision energy can be used to search for the CEP in experimental measurements
Stephanov et al. 1999
;
Stephanov 2009
;
Stephanov 2011
, cf. also
Luo and Xu 2017
. In the first phase of the Beam Energy Scan (BES-I) program at the Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider (RHIC) in the last decade, cumulants of net-proton, net-charge and net-kaon multiplicity distributions of different orders, and their correlations have been measured
Adamczyk et al. 2014a
;
Adamczyk et al. 2014b
;
Luo 2015
;
Adamczyk et al. 2018
;
Adam et al. 2019
;
Adam et al. 2021
;
Abdallah et al. 2021a
;
Abdallah et al. 2021b
. Notably, recently a non-monotonic dependence of the kurtosis of the net-proton multiplicity distribution on the collision energy is observed with
3.1\,\sigma
significance for central gold-on-gold (Au+Au) collisions
Adam et al. 2021
.

In this work we would like to present an overview on recent progress in studies of QCD at finite temperature and densities within the fRG approach. The fRG is a nonperturbative continuum field approach, in which quantum, thermal and density fluctuations are integrated in successively with the evolution of the renormalization group (RG) scale
Wetterich 1993
, cf. also
Ellwanger 1994
;
Morris 1994
. For QCD-related reviews, see, e.g.,
Berges et al. 2002
;
Pawlowski 2007
;
Schaefer and Wambach 2008
;
Gies 2012
;
Rosten 2012
;
Braun 2012
;
Pawlowski 2014
;
Dupuis et al. 2021
. Remarkably, recent years have seen significant progress in first-principle fRG calculations, for example, the state-of-the-art quantitative fRG results for Yang-Mills theory in the vacuum
Cyrol et al. 2016
and at finite temperature
Cyrol et al. 2018a
, vacuum QCD results in the quenched approximation
Mitter et al. 2015
, unquenched QCD in the vacuum
Braun et al. 2016a
;
Rennecke 2015a
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
and at finite temperature and densities
Fu et al. 2020a
;
Braun et al. 2020a
.

In this paper we try to present a self-contained overview, which include some fundamental derivations. Although new researchers in this field, e.g., students, may find these derivations useful, familiar readers could just skip over them. Furthermore, in this paper we focus on studies of fRG at finite temperature and densities, so we have to give up some topics, which in fact are very important for the developments and applications of the fRG approach, such as the quantitative fRG calculations to QCD in the vacuum
Mitter et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2016
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
.

This paper is organized as follows: In
Section

II
we introduce the formalism of the fRG approach, including the Wetterich equation, the flow equations of correlation functions, the technique of dynamical hadronization, etc. Moreover, we also give a brief discussion about the Wilson’s recursion formula and the Polchinski equation, which are closely related to the fRG approach. In
Section

III
we discuss the application of fRG in the low energy effective field theories (LEFTs), including the Nambu–Jona-Lasinio model and the quark-meson model. The relevant results in LEFTs, e.g., the phase structure, the equation of state, baryon number fluctuations, critical exponents, are presented. In
Section

IV
we turn to the application of fRG to QCD at finite temperature and densities. After a discussion about the flows of the propagators, strong couplings, four-quark couplings and the Yukawa couplings, we present and discuss the relevant results, e.g., the natural emergence of LEFTs from QCD, several different chiral condensates, QCD phase diagram and QCD phase structure, the inhomogeneous instability at large baryon chemical potentials, the magnetic equation of state, etc. In
Section

V
we discuss the real-time fRG. After the derivation of the fRG flows on the Schwinger-Keldysh closed time path, one formulates the real-time effective action in terms of the “classical” and “quantum” fields in the physical representation. The spectral functions and the dynamical critical exponent of the
O(N)
scalar theory are discussed. In
Section

VI
a summary with conclusions is given. Moreover, an example for the flow equations of the gluon and ghost self-energies in Yang-Mills theory at finite temperature is given in
Appendix

A
. The Fierz-complete basis of four-quark interactions of
N_{f}=2
flavors is listed in
Appendix

B
, and some useful flow functions are collected in
Appendix

C
.

II
Formalism of the fRG approach

We begin the section with a derivation of the Wetterich equation, i.e., the flow equation of the effective action, which is followed by a brief introduction about the Wilson’s recursion formula and the Polchinski equation, since they are closely related to the fRG approach. Then, the fRG approach is applied to QCD. A simple method to obtain the flow equations of correlation functions, i.e.,
Section

II.4.1
, is presented. An example for the flow equations of the gluon and ghost self-energies in Yang-Mills theory at finite temperature is given in
Appendix

A
. Finally, the technique of dynamical hadronization is discussed in
Section

II.5
.

II.1
Flow equation of the effective action

We begin with a generating functional for a classical action
S[\hat{\Phi}]
with an infrared (IR) regulator as follows

\displaystyle Z_{k}[J]=

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi})\exp\Big\{-S[\hat{\Phi}]-\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]+J^{a}\hat{\Phi}_{a}\Big\}\,,

(1)

where the field
\hat{\Phi}
is a collective symbol for all fields relevant in a specific physical problem, and the hat on the field is used to distinguish from its expected value in the following. It can even include fields which do not appear in the original classical action, and we will come back to this topic in what follows. The suffix of
\hat{\Phi}
,
a
, denotes not only the discrete degrees of freedom, e.g., the species, inner components of fields, etc., but also the continuous spacetime coordinates or the energy and momenta. The external source
J^{a}
is conjugated to
\hat{\Phi}_{a}
. A summation or/and integral is assumed for a repeated index as shown in
Equation

1
. The
k
-dependent regulator
\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]
in
Equation

1
is used to suppress quantum fluctuations of momenta
q\lesssim k
, while leave those of
q>k
untouched. Usually, a bilinear term, convenient in actual computations, is adopted for the regulator, which reads

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hat{\Phi}_{a}R^{ab}_{k}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\,,

(2)

with
R^{ab}_{k}=R^{ba}_{k}
for bosonic indices and
R^{ab}_{k}=-R^{ba}_{k}
for fermionic ones. See e.g.,
Pawlowski 2007
for discussions about generic regulators. We will see in the following that the IR cutoff
k
here is essentially the renormalization group (RG) scale.

We proceed to taking a single scalar field
\varphi
for example, and the relevant regulator reads

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\varphi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}xd^{4}y\varphi(x)R_{k}(x,y)\varphi(y)\,.

(3)

It is more convenient to work in the momentum space. Employing the Fourier transformation as follows

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\varphi(q)e^{iqx}\,,

(4)

\displaystyle R_{k}(x,y)=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}R_{k}(q)e^{iq(x-y)}\,,

(5)

one is led to

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\varphi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\varphi(-q)R_{k}(q)\varphi(q)\,.

(6)

Here, the regulator has the following asymptotic properties which read

\displaystyle R_{k\rightarrow\infty}(q)\rightarrow\infty,\quad\text{and}\quad R_{k\rightarrow 0}(q)\rightarrow 0\,,

(7)

with a fixed
q
. In order to fulfill the aforementioned requirement, viz., only suppressing quantum fluctuations of momenta
q\lesssim k
selectively, one could make the choice as follows

\displaystyle R_{k}(q)\big|_{q<k}\sim k^{2},\qquad R_{k}(q)\big|_{q>k}\sim 0\,.

(8)

One may have already noticed that there are infinite regulators fulfilling
Equation

8
. Here, we present two classes of regulators that are frequently used in literatures: One is the exponential-like regulator as follows

\displaystyle R_{k}^{\mathrm{exp},n}(q)

\displaystyle=q^{2}r_{\mathrm{exp},n}(q^{2}/k^{2})\,,

(9)

with

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{exp},n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{n-1}}{e^{x^{n}}-1}\,.

(10)

The sharpness of regulator in the vicinity of
q=k
is determined by the parameter
n
, as shown in
Figure

1
, where the exponential regulators with
n=1
and 2 are depicted as functions of
q
with a fixed
k
. In
Figure

1
we also plot another commonly used regulator, i.e., the flat or optimized one
Litim 2000
;
Litim 2001
, which reads

\displaystyle R_{k}^{\mathrm{opt}}(q)

\displaystyle=q^{2}r_{\mathrm{opt}}(q^{2}/k^{2})\,,

(11)

with

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{opt}}(x)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{x}-1\Big)\Theta(1-x)\,,

(12)

where
\Theta(x)
is the Heaviside step function. Moreover, the derivative of regulator with respect to the RG scale
k
, to wit,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}R_{k}(q)

\displaystyle\equiv k\partial_{k}R_{k}(q)\,,

(13)

is also shown in
Figure

1
, where
t
is usually called as the RG time. For more discussions about regulators, see, e.g.,
Fister and Pawlowski 2015
.

Figure 1:
Comparison of several different regulators and their derivative with respect to the RG scale
k
as functions of the momentum
q
with a fixed
k
.

It is more convenient to use the generating functional for connected correlation functions, viz.,

\displaystyle W_{k}[J]

\displaystyle=\ln Z_{k}[J]\,,

(14)

which is also known as the Schwinger function. Then, the expected value of a field is readily obtained as

\displaystyle\Phi_{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\rangle=\frac{\delta W_{k}[J]}{\delta J^{a}}\,,

(15)

and the propagator reads

\displaystyle G_{k,ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle_{c}=\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle-\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\rangle\langle\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{2}W_{k}[J]}{\delta J^{a}J^{b}}\,,

(16)

where the subscript
c
stands for “connected”. Moreover, Legendre transformation to the Schwinger function leaves us immediately with the one particle irreducible (1PI) effective action, which reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=-W_{k}[J]+J^{a}\Phi_{a}-\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]\,.

(17)

In order to take both bosonic and fermionic fields into account all together, we adopt the notation introduced in
Pawlowski 2007
, to wit,

\displaystyle J^{a}\Phi_{a}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}\Phi_{a}J^{b}\,,

(18)

with

\displaystyle\gamma^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}-\delta^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb},\quad\text{a and b are fermionic,}\\
\delta^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb},\quad\hskip 7.11317pt\text{others.}\end{array}\right.

Inserting
Equation

18
into
Equation

17
and differentiating both sides of
Equation

17
with respect to
\Phi_{a}
, one is led to

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}[\Phi])}{\delta\Phi_{a}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}J^{b}\,,

(21)

whereby, the propagator in
Equation

16
is readily written as the inverse of the second-order derivative of
\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]
w.r.t.
\Phi
, i.e.,

\displaystyle G_{k,ab}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{c}_{\hskip 4.26773pta}\Big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\Big)^{-1}_{cb}\,,

(22)

with

\displaystyle\Big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\Big)^{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta^{2}(\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}[\Phi])}{\delta\Phi_{a}\delta\Phi_{b}}\,.

(23)

We proceed to considering the evolution of Schwinger function with the RG scale, i.e., the flow equation of
W_{k}[J]
, which is straightforwardly obtained from Eqs. (
1
) and (
14
). The resulting flow reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}W_{k}[J]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\text{STr}\Big[\big(\partial_{t}R_{k}\big)G_{k}\Big]-\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{a}\partial_{t}R^{ab}_{k}\Phi_{b}\,,

(24)

where we have introduced a notation super trace for compactness, which can also be expressed as

\displaystyle\text{STr}\Big[\big(\partial_{t}R_{k}\big)G_{k}\Big]

\displaystyle=\big(\partial_{t}R^{ab}_{k}\big)\gamma^{c}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}G_{k,ca}\,.

(25)

The factor
\gamma
in the equation above indicates that the super trace provides an additional minus sign for fermionic degrees of freedom. Note that in deriving
Equation

24
we have used the relation in
Equation

16
. Applying Legendre transformation in
Equation

17
to the flow equation of Schwinger function in
Equation

24
once more, one immediately arrives at the flow equation of the effective action, as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}W_{k}[J]-\partial_{t}\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\text{STr}\Big[\big(\partial_{t}R_{k}\big)G_{k}\Big]\,,

(26)

which is the Wetterich equation
Wetterich 1993
. Note that the flow equation of effective action in
Equation

26
would be modified when the dynamical hadronization is encoded, see
Equation

83
. In
Section

II.3
we would like to give an example for the application of fRG, and postpone discussions of the dynamical hadronization in
Section

II.5
.

II.2
From Wilson’s RG to Polchinski equation

The idea encoded in the Wetterich equation in
Equation

26
is that, integrating out high momentum modes leaves us with a RG rescaled theory, and this theory is invariant at a second-order phase transition. This idea is also reflected in the Wilson’s RG and Polchinski equation, to be discussed in this subsection.

II.2.1
Wilson’s RG and recursion formula

Here we follow
Ma 2000
and begin with the Ginzburg-Landau Hamiltonian
\mathcal{K}\equiv H[\sigma]/T
, which reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=

\displaystyle\int d^{d}x\Big[\frac{1}{2}c\big(\nabla\sigma\big)^{2}+U(\sigma)\Big]\,.

(27)

Then one separates the field into two parts as follows

\displaystyle\sigma=

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}+\tilde{\sigma}\,,

(28)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle L^{-d/2}\sum_{\Lambda/2<q<\Lambda}\sigma_{\bm{q}}e^{i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{x}}\,,

(29)

where
L
denotes the size of the system,
\Lambda
is a UV cutoff scale and
\Lambda^{-1}
can be regarded as the lattice spacing. The plane-wave expansion in
Equation

29
can also be replaced by that in terms of localized wave packets
W_{z}(\bm{x})
, i.e., the Wannier functions for the band of plane waves
\Lambda/2<q<\Lambda
, that is,

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\sum_{z}\tilde{\sigma}_{z}W_{z}(\bm{x})\,.

(30)

Obviously, the wave packets have the property
W_{z}(\bm{x})\sim 0
, if
|\bm{x}-\bm{z}|\gg 2\Lambda^{-1}
, and one also has

\displaystyle\int d^{d}xW_{z}(\bm{x})W_{z^{\prime}}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle\delta_{zz^{\prime}}\,,

(31)

i.e., they are orthonormal. Substituting
Equation

28
into
Equation

27
and performing a functional integral over
\tilde{\sigma}(\bm{x})
or
\tilde{\sigma}_{z}
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{D}\tilde{\sigma})e^{-\mathcal{K}}=\exp\Big[-\int d^{d}x\frac{1}{2}c\big(\nabla\sigma^{\prime}\big)^{2}\Big]\prod_{z}I(\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\bigg\{-\int d^{d}x\Big[\frac{1}{2}c\big(\nabla\sigma^{\prime}\big)^{2}+\bar{U}^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})\Big]-AL^{d}\bigg\}\,,

(32)

with

\displaystyle I(\sigma^{\prime})\equiv

\displaystyle\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\int dy\exp\bigg\{-\frac{c}{2}\bar{q^{2}}\Omega y^{2}-\frac{\Omega}{2}\Big[U(\sigma^{\prime}+y)

\displaystyle+U(\sigma^{\prime}-y)\Big]\bigg\}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\exp\Big[-\Omega\bar{U}^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})-\Omega A\Big]\,,

(33)

where
\Omega
is the volume of a block, or the wave packet; the constant
A
is determined by the condition
\bar{U}^{\prime}(0)=0
; The mean square wave vector of the packet reads

\displaystyle\bar{q^{2}}=

\displaystyle\int d^{d}x\Big(\nabla W_{z}(\bm{x})\Big)^{2}\,.

(34)

In deriving
Equation

32
, one has neglected the overlap between wave packets, the variation of
\sigma^{\prime}(\bm{x})
and the absolute value of
W_{z}(\bm{x})
within a block, and see
Wilson 1971a
;
Wilson 1971b
;
Ma 2000
for details. Note that
Equation

32
is just the first step of the Kadanoff transformation.

The second step of Kadanoff transformation is to make replacement for the remaining field
\sigma^{\prime}
and the coordinate in
Equation

32
as follows

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}(\bm{x})\rightarrow

\displaystyle 2^{1-\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}\sigma(\bm{x}^{\prime})\,,\qquad\bm{x}^{\prime}=\frac{\bm{x}}{2}\,,

(35)

and thus one is left with

\displaystyle\mathcal{K^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\int d^{d}x^{\prime}\Big[\frac{1}{2}c^{\prime}\big(\nabla\sigma\big)^{2}+U^{\prime}(\sigma)\Big]\,,

(36)

with

\displaystyle c^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2^{-\eta}c\,,

(37)

and

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(\sigma)=

\displaystyle-2^{d}\Omega^{-1}\ln\frac{I(2^{1-\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\eta}{2}}\sigma)}{I(0)}\,.

(38)

In order to make
c^{\prime}=c
satisfied, one adopts
\eta=0
. Choosing an appropriate value of
c
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{c}{2}\bar{q^{2}}\Omega=

\displaystyle 1\,,

(39)

and defining

\displaystyle Q(\sigma)\equiv

\displaystyle\Omega U(\sigma)\,,

(40)

one arrives at

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(\sigma)=

\displaystyle-2^{d}\ln\frac{I(2^{1-\frac{d}{2}}\sigma)}{I(0)}\,.

(41)

with

\displaystyle I(\sigma)=

\displaystyle\int dy\exp\bigg\{-y^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big[Q(\sigma+y)+Q(\sigma-y)\Big]\bigg\}\,,

(42)

which is the Wilson’s recursion formula
Wilson 1971a
;
Wilson 1971b
. Note that the constant prefactor
\Omega^{1/2}
in
Equation

33
is irrelevant.

II.2.2
Polchinski equation

In
Section

II.2.1
we have discussed the viewpoint of Wilson’s RG, that is, integrating out modes of high scales successively leaves us with a low energy theory that evolves with the RG scale. This idea is also applied to a generic quantum field theory within the formalism of the functional integral, due to Polchinski
Polchinski 1984
. We begin with a generating functional for a scalar field theory in four Euclidean dimensions with a momentum cutoff, i.e.,

\displaystyle Z[J]=

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{D}\phi)\exp\bigg\{\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\phi(p)\phi(-p)(p^{2}+m^{2})

\displaystyle\times K^{-1}\big(\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\big)+J(p)\phi(-p)\Big]+L_{\mathrm{int}}(\phi)\bigg\}\,,

(43)

with a cutoff function given by

\displaystyle K(p^{2}/\Lambda_{0}^{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}1,\quad p^{2}<\Lambda_{0}^{2},\\
0,\quad p^{2}\gg\Lambda_{0}^{2}.\end{array}\right.

Evidently, here
\Lambda_{0}
plays a role as a UV cutoff scale, and
L_{\mathrm{int}}
in
Equation

43
is the interaction Lagrangian at the scale
\Lambda_{0}
, that for example reads

\displaystyle L_{\mathrm{int}}(\phi)=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{0}_{1}\phi^{2}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{0}_{2}\big(\partial_{\mu}\phi(x)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4!}\rho^{0}_{3}\phi^{4}(x)\Big]\,,

(46)

where we have used the notation in
Polchinski 1984
, and
\rho^{0}_{a}
’s stand for bare quantities.

One would like to integrate out the high momentum modes of
\phi
and reduce the UV cutoff
\Lambda_{0}
to a lower value, say
\Lambda
. In the meantime, one chooses
|m^{2}|<\Lambda
and
J(p)=0
for
p^{2}>\Lambda^{2}
. As we have discussed in
Section

II.2.1
, when high momentum modes are integrated out, new effective interactions, included in the potential
U^{\prime}
in
Equation

36
or the interaction Lagrangian
L_{\mathrm{int}}
in
Equation

43
, are generated. Thus, one is led to the following functional integral

\displaystyle Z[J,L,\Lambda]=

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{D}\phi)\exp\bigg\{\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\phi(p)\phi(-p)(p^{2}

\displaystyle+m^{2})K^{-1}\big(\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\big)+J(p)\phi(-p)\Big]+L(\phi,\Lambda)\bigg\}\,.

(47)

If one wishes to take the generating functional
Z[J,L,\Lambda]
on the l.h.s. of the equation above to be independent of the scale
\Lambda
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Lambda\frac{dZ[J,L,\Lambda]}{d\Lambda}=

\displaystyle 0\,,

(48)

the following evolution equation for the interaction Lagrangian in
Equation

47
has to be satisfied, to wit,

\displaystyle\Lambda\frac{\partial L(\phi,\Lambda)}{\partial\Lambda}=

\displaystyle-\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{p^{2}+m^{2}}\Lambda\frac{\partial K\big(\frac{p^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\big)}{\partial\Lambda}

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\frac{\delta^{2}L}{\delta\phi(p)\delta\phi(-p)}+\frac{\delta L}{\delta\phi(p)}\frac{\delta L}{\delta\phi(-p)}\bigg]\,,

(49)

which is the Polchinski equation
Polchinski 1984
.

II.3
Application to QCD

Figure 2:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equation for QCD effective action. The lines denote full propagators for the gluon, ghost, quark, and meson, respectively. The crossed circles stand for the infrared regulators.

In this section we would like to apply the formalism of fRG discussed above to an effective action of rebosonized QCD in
Fu et al. 2020a
. The truncation for the Euclidean effective action reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{x}\bigg\{\frac{1}{4}F^{a}_{\mu\nu}F^{a}_{\mu\nu}+Z_{c,k}\big(\partial_{\mu}\bar{c}^{a}\big)D_{\mu}^{ab}c^{b}+\frac{1}{2\xi}\big(\partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\mu}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle+Z_{q,k}\bar{q}\big(\gamma_{\mu}D_{\mu}-\gamma_{0}\hat{\mu}\big)q+m_{s}(\sigma_{s})\bar{q}_{s}q_{s}-\lambda_{q,k}\Big[\big(\bar{q}_{l}\,T^{0}q_{l}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\big(\bar{q}_{l}\,i\gamma_{5}\bm{T}q_{l}\big)^{2}\Big]+h_{k}\bar{q}_{l}\big(T^{0}\sigma+i\gamma_{5}\bm{T}\cdot\bm{\pi}\big)q_{l}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}Z_{\phi,k}(\partial_{\mu}\phi)^{2}+V_{k}(\rho,A_{0})-c_{\sigma}\,\sigma-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,c_{\sigma_{s}}\,\sigma_{s}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Delta\Gamma_{\mathrm{glue}}\,,

(50)

with
\int_{x}=\int_{0}^{1/T}dx_{0}\int d^{3}x
,
T
being the temperature. One can see that field contents in
Equation

50
include not only the fundamental fields in QCD, i.e., the gluon, Faddeev-Popov ghost, and the quark, but also the composite fields
\phi=(\sigma,\bm{\pi})
, the scalar and pseudo-scalar mesons respectively. Note that here the mesonic fields are not added by hands, but rather dynamically generated and transferred from the fundamental degrees of freedom via the dynamical hadronization technique, described in detail in
Section

II.5
. Consequently, there is no double counting for the degrees of freedom. In short, one is left with
\Phi=(A,c,\bar{c},q,\bar{q},\sigma,\pi)
. The
h_{k}
and
\lambda_{q,k}
in
Equation

50
denote the Yukawa coupling and the four-quark coupling, respectively,

The first line on the r.h.s. of
Equation

50
denotes the classical action for the glue sector, while its non-classical contributions are collected in
\Delta\Gamma_{\mathrm{glue}}
. The gauge parameter
\xi=0
, i.e., the Landau gauge, is commonly adopted in the computation of functional approaches. The wave function renormalization
Z_{\Phi,k}
of field
\Phi
is defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\Phi}

\displaystyle=Z_{\Phi,k}^{1/2}\Phi\,,

(51)

with the renormalized field
\bar{\Phi}
. The gluonic field strength tensor reads

\displaystyle F^{a}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=Z_{A,k}^{1/2}\big(\partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A^{a}_{\mu}+Z_{A,k}^{1/2}\bar{g}_{\text{\tiny glue},k}f^{abc}A^{b}_{\mu}A^{c}_{\nu}\big)\,.

(52)

Note that although different strong couplings are identical in the perturbative region, they can deviate from each other in the nonperturbative or even semiperturbative regime
Mitter et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2016
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
;
Cyrol et al. 2018a
, and see also relevant discussions in
Section

IV.2
. Therefore, it is necessary to distinguish different strong couplings. The renormalized strong couplings in the glue sector read

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{A^{3},k}=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{A^{3},k}}{Z_{A,k}^{3/2}}\,,\quad\bar{g}_{A^{4},k}=\frac{\lambda_{A^{4},k}^{1/2}}{Z_{A,k}}\,,\quad\bar{g}_{\bar{c}cA,k}=\frac{\lambda_{\bar{c}cA,k}}{Z_{A,k}^{1/2}\,Z_{c,k}}\,,

(53)

where
\lambda_{A^{3},k}
,
\lambda_{A^{4},k}
and
\lambda_{\bar{c}cA,k}
stand for the three-gluon, four-gluon, ghost-gluon dressing functions, respectively, as shown in
Equation

363
,
Equation

369
,
Equation

357
. In
Equation

52
the gluonic strong couplings are denoted collectively as
\bar{g}_{\text{\tiny glue},k}
. In the same way, the quark-gluon coupling reads

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{\bar{q}qA,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\bar{q}qA,k}}{Z_{A,k}^{1/2}\,Z_{q,k}}\,,

(54)

with the quark-gluon dressing function
\lambda_{\bar{q}qA,k}
. The covariant derivative in the fundamental and adjoint representations of the color
SU(N_{c})
group reads

\displaystyle D_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}-iZ_{A,k}^{1/2}\bar{g}_{\bar{q}qA,k}A^{a}_{\mu}t^{a}\,,

(55)

\displaystyle D^{ab}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\delta^{ab}-Z_{A,k}^{1/2}\bar{g}_{\bar{c}cA,k}f^{abc}A^{c}_{\mu}\,,

(56)

respectively. Here
f^{abc}
is the antisymmetric structure constant of the
SU(N_{c})
group, determined from its Lie algebra
[t^{a},t^{b}]=if^{abc}t^{c}
, where the generators have the normalization
\operatorname{Tr}t^{a}t^{b}=(1/2)\delta^{ab}
.

In
Equation

50
the formalism of
N_{f}=3
flavor quark is built upon that of
N_{f}=2
by means of addition of a dynamical strange quark
q_{s}
, whose constituent quark mass
m_{s}(\sigma_{s})
is determined self-consistently from a extended effective potential of
SU(N_{f}=2)
, and see
Fu et al. 2020a
for more details. Therefore, we have the quark field
q=(q_{l},q_{s})
, where the
u
and
d
light quarks are denoted by
q_{l}=(q_{u},q_{d})
. The light quarks interact with themself through the four-quark coupling in the
\sigma-\pi
channel, and they are also coupled with the
\sigma
and
\pi
mesons via the Yukawa coupling. Here
T^{i}
(
i=1
, 2, 3) are the generators of the group
SU(N_{f}=2)
in the flavor space with
\operatorname{Tr}T^{i}T^{j}=(1/2)\delta^{ij}
and
T^{0}=(1/\sqrt{2N_{f}})\mathbb{1}_{N_{f}\times N_{f}}
with
N_{f}=2
. In
Equation

50

\hat{\mu}=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_{u},\mu_{d},\mu_{s})
stands for the matrix of quark chemical potentials.

The effective potential in
Equation

50
can be decomposed into two parts as follows

\displaystyle V_{k}(\rho,A_{0})

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{glue},k}(A_{0})+V_{\mathrm{mat},k}(\rho,A_{0})\,.

(57)

The first term on the r.h.s. of equation above is the glue potential, or the Polyakov loop potential. The temporal gluon field
A_{0}
is intimately related to the Polyakov loop
L[A_{0}]
, see e.g.,
Herbst et al. 2015
; the second term is the mesonic effective potential of
N_{f}=2
flavors, which is
O(4)
-invariant with
\rho=\phi^{2}/2
. Moreover, in the effective action in
Equation

50

c_{\sigma}
and
c_{\sigma_{s}}
are the parameters of explicit chiral symmetry breaking for the light and strange scalars
\sigma
,
\sigma_{s}
, respectively.

Applying the fRG flow equation in
Equation

26
to the QCD effective action in
Equation

50
, one immediately arrives at the QCD flow equation, which is depicted in
Figure

2
. One can see that the flow of effective potential receives contributions from the gluon, ghost, quark, and composite fields separately, each of which is one-loop structure. It should be emphasized that, although it is a one-loop structure, the flow is composed of full propagators which are in turn dependent on the second derivative of the effective action, see
Equation

22
. Thus, the flow equation in
Figure

2
a functional self-consistent differential equation, which will be explored further in the following.

II.4
Flow equations of correlation functions

Combining
Equation

22
one can reformulate Wetterich equation in
Equation

26
slightly such that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{STr}\Big[\tilde{\partial_{t}}\ln\big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+R_{k}\big)\Big]\,,

(58)

where the differential operator with a tilde,
\tilde{\partial_{t}}
, hits the RG-scale dependence only through the regulator. Note that
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]
in
Equation

58
is a bit different from that in
Equation

23
, and here the factor
\gamma^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}
is absorbed in
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]
. A convenient way to take this into account is to use the definition as follows

\displaystyle\big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\big)^{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\overrightarrow{\delta}}{\delta\Phi_{a}}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]\frac{\overleftarrow{\delta}}{\delta\Phi_{b}}\,,

(59)

where the left and right derivatives have been adopted.

In order to derive the flow equations for various correlation functions of different orders, it is useful to express
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}
in
Equation

59
in terms of a matrix, and the indices of matrix correspond to different fields involved in the theory concerned, e.g.,
\Phi=(A,c,\bar{c},q,\bar{q},\sigma,\pi)
in
Section

II.3
. This matrix is also called as the fluctuation matrix
Gies and Wetterich 2002
. Then, one can make the division as follows

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}+R_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{F}\,,

(60)

where
\mathcal{P}
is the matrix of two-point correlation functions including the regulators, and its inverse gives rise to propagators;
\mathcal{F}
is the matrix of interaction which includes
n
-point correlation functions with
n>2
, and thus terms in
\mathcal{F}
have the field dependence. Substituting
Equation

60
into
Equation

58
and making the Taylor expansion in order of
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{P}
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{STr}\Big[\tilde{\partial}_{t}\ln(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{F})\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{STr}\tilde{\partial}_{t}\ln\mathcal{P}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{STr}\tilde{\partial}_{t}\Big(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{F}\Big)-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{STr}\tilde{\partial}_{t}\Big(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{F}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}\mathrm{STr}\tilde{\partial}_{t}\Big(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{F}\Big)^{3}-\frac{1}{8}\mathrm{STr}\tilde{\partial}_{t}\Big(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{F}\Big)^{4}+\cdots\,,

(61)

from which it is straightforward to obtain the flow equations for various inverse propagators and vertices at some appropriate orders.

Moreover, one can also employ some well-developed Mathematica packages, e.g., DoFun
Huber and Braun 2012
;
Huber et al. 2020
, QMeS-Derivation
Pawlowski et al. 2021
, to derive the flow equations for correlation functions. We refrain from elaborating on details about usage of these Mathematica packages in this review, which interested readers can find in the references above, but rather would like to introduce another easy-to-use approach to derive the flow equations described in detail in
Section

II.4.1
.

II.4.1
A simple approach to derivation of flow equations of correlation functions

Figure 3:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equation for the gluon self-energy in Yang-Mills theory, where the last diagram arises from the non-classical two-ghost–two-gluon vertex.

We proceed with defining a generic 1PI
n
-point correlation function or vertex, as follows

\displaystyle V_{k,\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\Phi_{a_{n}}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\equiv-\Gamma_{k,\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\Phi_{a_{n}}}^{(n)}=-\left.\left(\frac{\delta^{n}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\delta\Phi_{a_{n}}}\right)\right|_{\Phi=\langle\Phi\rangle}\,,

(62)

where
\langle\Phi\rangle
denotes the value of
\Phi
on its equation of motion (EoM). The flow equation of vertex
V_{k}^{(n)}
in
Equation

62
can be represented schematically as the equation as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{k,\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\Phi_{a_{n}}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\partial}_{t}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{all\;one\!-\!loop\;correction}\\
\mathrm{diagrams\;of\;}V_{k,\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\Phi_{a_{n}}}^{(n)}\end{array}\right)\,.

Note that the one-loop diagrams on the r.h.s. are comprised of full propagators and vertices. As we have mentioned above, the partial derivative with a tilde
\tilde{\partial_{t}}
hits the RG-scale dependence only through the regulator, and thus its implementation on diagrams would give rise to the insertion of a regulator for each inner propagator.

As an example, we present the flow equation of the gluon self-energy in Yang-Mills theory in
Figure

3
. One can see that it receives contributions from the gluon loop, the tadpole of the gluon, the ghost loop, and the tadpole of the ghost. Note that the last diagram on the r.h.s. of equation in
Figure

3
arises from the non-classical two-ghost–two-gluon vertex. Remarkably, the factors in front of each diagram are in agreement with those in the perturbation theory. It, however, should be emphasized once more that although these diagrams are very similar with the formalism of perturbation theory, they are essentially nonperturbative, since both propagators and vertices in these diagrams are the full ones.

In order to let readers be familiar with the computation in fRG, we present some details about the flow equations of the gluon and ghost self-energies in Yang-Mills theory at finite temperature in
Appendix

A
.

II.5
Dynamical hadronization

In
Section

II.3
we have mentioned that the mesonic fields in
Equation

50
are not added by hands. On the contrary, these composite degrees of freedom are dynamically generated from fundamental ones with the evolution of the RG scale from the ultraviolet (UV) toward infrared (IR) limit. This is done via a technique called the dynamical hadronization, which was proposed in
Gies and Wetterich 2002
;
Gies and Wetterich 2004
, and subsequently the formalism was further developed in
Pawlowski 2007
. Notably, recently the explicit chiral symmetry breaking and its role within the dynamical hadronization have been investigated in detail in
Fu et al. 2020a
, and a flow of dynamical hadronization with manifest chiral symmetry is put forward therein. In this section, we follow
Fu et al. 2020a
and present the derivation of flow equation of the dynamical hadronization.

We denote the original or fundamental degrees of freedom in QCD as
\hat{\varphi}=(\hat{A},\hat{c},\hat{\bar{c}},\hat{q},\hat{\bar{q}})
with the expected value
\varphi=\langle\hat{\varphi}\rangle
, whereas composite degrees of freedom are introduced via a RG scale
k
-dependent composite field
\hat{\phi}_{k}(\hat{\varphi})

Gies and Wetterich 2002
;
Gies and Wetterich 2004
;
Pawlowski 2007
, which is a function of the fundamental field
\hat{\varphi}
. Then the superfield reads

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=(\varphi,\phi_{k})=(A,c,\bar{c},q,\bar{q},\phi_{k})\,,

(65)

with

\displaystyle\phi_{k}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{\phi}_{k}(\hat{\varphi})\rangle\,.

(66)

The generating functional in
Equation

1
is modified a bit such that

\displaystyle Z_{k}[J]=\exp\big(W_{k}[J]\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int(\mathcal{D}\hat{\varphi})\exp\Big\{-S_{\mathrm{QCD}}[\hat{\varphi}]-\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\varphi},\hat{\phi}_{k}]+J_{\varphi}\cdot\hat{\varphi}

\displaystyle+J_{\phi}\cdot\hat{\phi}_{k}\Big\}\,,

(67)

where the external source
J=(J_{\varphi},J_{\phi})
with
J_{\varphi}=(J_{A},J_{c},J_{\bar{c}},J_{q},J_{\bar{q}})
is conjugated to the field
\Phi=(\varphi,\phi_{k})
, distinguished with different labels of indices, i.e.,

\displaystyle J\cdot\hat{\Phi}

\displaystyle=J^{a}\hat{\Phi}_{a},\quad J_{\varphi}\cdot\hat{\varphi}=J_{\varphi}^{\alpha}\hat{\varphi}_{\alpha},\quad J_{\phi}\cdot\hat{\phi}_{k}=J_{\phi}^{i}\hat{\phi}_{k,i}\,.

(68)

The regulator of bilinear fields in
Equation

67
reads

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\varphi},\hat{\phi}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\Phi}_{a}R^{ab}_{k}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\,.

(69)

The effective action is obtained via a Legendre transformation to the Schwinger function as shown in
Equation

17
. Note that the term of explicit chiral symmetry breaking in the effective action, e.g.,
-c_{\sigma}\sigma
or
-c_{\sigma_{s}}\sigma_{s}/\sqrt{2}
in
Equation

50
, does not contribute to the flow of effective action, and thus the effective action can always be decomposed into that in the case of chiral limit plus an explicit chiral symmetry breaking term. We follow
Fu et al. 2020a
and separate the explicit breaking out, such that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]-c_{\sigma}\sigma\,,

(70)

where we have concentrated on the case of
N_{f}=2
, that can be easily extended to include the strange quark.
\bar{\Gamma}_{k}
in
Equation

70
stands for the effective action without the explicit chiral symmetry breaking. From
Equation

70
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]-J\cdot\Phi

\displaystyle=\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]-c_{\sigma}\sigma-J\cdot\Phi

\displaystyle=\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]-\bar{J}\cdot\Phi\,,

(71)

with

\displaystyle\bar{J}

\displaystyle=(J_{\varphi},J_{\sigma}+c_{\sigma},J_{\pi})\,,

(72)

i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=J_{\sigma}+c_{\sigma}\,,\quad\bar{J}_{\varphi}=J_{\varphi}\,,\quad\bar{J}_{\pi}=J_{\pi}\,.

(73)

Equation

71
combined with
Equation

17
leaves us with the relation for Schwinger functions as follows

\displaystyle W_{k}[J]

\displaystyle=\bar{W}_{k}[\bar{J}]\,,

(74)

where one has

\displaystyle\bar{W}_{k}[J]

\displaystyle=W_{k}[J]\Big|_{c_{\sigma}\rightarrow 0}\,,

(75)

that is,
\bar{W}_{k}
denotes the Schwinger function in the absence of the explicit chiral symmetry breaking. Similar with
Equation

26
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\bar{W}_{k}[\bar{J}]-\partial_{t}\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]\,,

(76)

where
\bar{W}_{k}[\bar{J}]=\ln\bar{Z}_{k}[\bar{J}]
can be obtained in
Equation

67
with
\bar{J}
in lieu of
J
and a chiral symmetric
S_{\mathrm{QCD}}[\hat{\varphi}]
. Subsequently, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{W}_{k}[\bar{J}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{Z}_{k}[\bar{J}]}\int(\mathcal{D}\hat{\varphi})\big(-\partial_{t}\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]+\bar{J}_{\phi}\cdot\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k}\big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big\{-S_{\mathrm{QCD}}[\hat{\varphi}]-\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]+\bar{J}_{\varphi}\cdot\hat{\varphi}+\bar{J}_{\phi}\cdot\hat{\phi}_{k}\Big\}\,.

(77)

It is straightforward to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\Phi}_{a}\big(\partial_{t}R^{ab}_{k}\big)\hat{\Phi}_{b}+\hat{\phi}_{k,i}R^{ij}_{k,\phi}\big(\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,j}\big)\,.

(78)

Inserting
Equation

78
into
Equation

77
and then to
Equation

76
, one is led to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}G_{k,ab}\partial_{t}R^{ab}_{k}+\langle\hat{\phi}_{k,i}R^{ij}_{k,\phi}\big(\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,j}\big)\rangle

\displaystyle-\bar{J}_{\phi}^{i}\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,i}\rangle\,,

(79)

where
Equation

16
has been used. Employing

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\phi}_{k,i}R^{ij}_{k,\phi}\big(\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,j}\big)\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big(G_{k,ia}\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi_{a}}+\phi_{k,i}\Big)R^{ij}_{k,\phi}\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,j}\rangle\,,

(80)

and

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{\phi}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\big(\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]\big)}{\delta\phi_{k,i}}\,,

(81)

one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}G_{k,ab}\partial_{t}R^{ab}_{k}+G_{k,ia}\Big(\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi_{a}}\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,j}\rangle\Big)R^{ij}_{k,\phi}

\displaystyle-\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,i}\rangle\frac{\delta\bar{\Gamma}_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\phi_{k,i}}\,.

(82)

Given the relation in
Equation

71
and the fact that
c_{\sigma}
is independent of the RG scale
k
, we finally obtain the flow equation of effective action with dynamical hadronization, to wit,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{STr}\big(G_{k}[\Phi]\,\partial_{t}R_{k}\big)+\mathrm{Tr}\bigg(G_{\phi\Phi_{a}}[\Phi]\frac{\delta\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k}\rangle}{\delta\Phi_{a}}\,R_{\phi}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\int\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k,i}\rangle\,\left(\frac{\delta\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\phi_{i}}+c_{\sigma}\delta_{i\,\sigma}\right)\,,

(83)

where some summations for the indices
\{a\}
and
\{i\}
as shown in
Equation

68
have been replaced with the super trace and trace, respectively; the integral over the spacetime coordinate is recovered for the last term on the r.h.s. of
Equation

83
. The propagator
G_{k,ia}
in
Equation

82
is relabeled with
G_{k,\phi_{i}\Phi_{a}}
that has a clearer physical meaning. One can see that in comparison to
Equation

26
, there are two additional terms, i.e., the last two on the r.h.s. of
Equation

83
, in the flow equation of the effective action. These additional terms arise from the RG scale dependent composite field in
Equation

66
, and they can be employed to implement the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation for every value of the RG scale, which eventually transfers the degrees of freedom from quarks to bound states.

III
Low energy effective field theories

Prior to discussing properties of the QCD matter at finite temperature and densities in
Section

IV
, in this section we would like to apply the formalism of fRG in
Section

II
to low energy effective field theories (LEFTs) firstly. We adopt two commonly used formalisms of LEFTs, i.e., the purely fermionic Nambu–Jona-Lasinio (NJL) model in
Section

III.1
and the quark-meson (QM) model in
Section

III.2
, respectively.

III.1
Nambu–Jona-Lasinio model

Figure 4:
Schematic representation of the flow equation for the four-quark coupling. Those on the r.h.s. of equation stand for three classes of diagrams contributing to the flow of four-quark interaction, that is, the two-gluon exchange, purely self-interacting four-quark coupling, and mixture of the quark-gluon and four-quark interactions, respectively. Here, diagrams of different channels of momenta are not distinguished and prefactors for each diagram are not shown.

Figure 5:
Left panel: Sketch of a typical
\beta
function for the dimensionless four-quark coupling
\bar{\lambda}\equiv k^{2}\lambda
in different cases, where
g
is the gauge coupling and
T
is the temperature. The plot is adopted from
Braun and Gies 2006
.
Right panel: Comparison between the
\beta
functions of
\bar{\lambda}
with and without an external magnetic field. The inclusion of a magnetic field results in that the chiral symmetry is always broken due to the dimensional reduction. The plot is adopted from
Fukushima and Pawlowski 2012
.

One prominent feature characteristic to the nonperturbative QCD is the dynamical chiral symmetry breaking, which is regarded as being responsible for the origin of the
\sim 98\%
mass of visible matter in the universe
Nambu and Jona-Lasinio 1961a
;
Nambu and Jona-Lasinio 1961b
;
Marciano and Pagels 1978
;
Roberts 2021
, in contradistinction to the
\sim 2\%
electroweak mass. Within the fRG approach, the dynamical breaking or restoration of the chiral symmetry is well encoded in the four-fermion flows, that is illustrated briefly in what follows. For more details, see, e.g.,
Fukushima and Pawlowski 2012
;
Aoki et al. 2016
;
Springer et al. 2017
;
Fu and Liu 2017
;
Aoki et al. 2018
;
Braun et al. 2017
;
Braun et al. 2018
;
Braun et al. 2020b
;
Leonhardt et al. 2020
;
Braun and Schallmo 2022
and a related review
Braun 2012
.

Using the method to derive the flow equation for a generic vertex as shown in
Section

II.4.1
, one is able to obtain the flow equation of four-quark coupling, depicted schematically in
Figure

4
. Here we refrain from going into the details of a realistic calculation, but rather try to infer behaviors of the four-quark flow connected to breaking or restoration of the chiral symmetry. It follows from
Figure

4
that the
\beta
function for the dimensionless four-quark coupling
\bar{\lambda}\equiv k^{2}\lambda
reads

\displaystyle\beta\equiv

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\lambda}=(d-2)\bar{\lambda}-a\bar{\lambda}^{2}-b\bar{\lambda}g^{2}-cg^{4}\,,

(84)

with the dimension of spacetime
d=4
and the strong coupling
g
. Note that apart from the first term on the r.h.s. of
Equation

84
arising from the dimension of
\lambda
, the remaining three terms corresponds to the three classes of diagrams in
Figure

5
one by one, and their coefficients are denoted by
a
,
b
,
c
, respectively. Further computation indicates one has
a>0
and
c>0

Braun and Gies 2006
.

In the left panel of
Figure

5
, a typical
\beta
function is plotted as a function of the dimensionless four-quark coupling schematically in different cases. When the gauge coupling
g
is vanishing and at zero temperature, there are two fixed points: One is the Gaussian IR fixed point
\bar{\lambda}=0
, the other the UV attractive fixed point at a nonvanishing
\bar{\lambda}
, and they are shown in the plot by red and purple dots, respectively. The position of the UV fixed point determines a critical value
\bar{\lambda}_{c}
, which is necessitated in order to break the chiral symmetry, since only when
\bar{\lambda}>\bar{\lambda}_{c}
, the four-quark coupling grows large and eventually diverges with the decreasing RG scale. When the temperature is turned on, the IR fixed point remains at the origin while the UV fixed point move towards right, as shown by the red dashed line. As a consequence, the broken chiral symmetry in the vacuum is restored at a finite
T
, if one has a value of
\bar{\lambda}
with
\bar{\lambda}_{c}(T=0)<\bar{\lambda}<\bar{\lambda}_{c}(T)
. When the strong coupling is nonzero, the parabola of
\beta
function moves downwards globally as shown by the blue curve in the left panel of
Figure

5
. There is a critical value of the strong coupling, say
g_{c}
, once one has
g>g_{c}
, the whole curve of the beta function is below the line
\beta=0
, which implies that the chiral symmetry breaking is bound to occur, no matter how large the initial value of
\bar{\lambda}
is.

The four-fermion flow is also well suited for an analysis of the chiral symmetry breaking in an external magnetic field. The inclusion of a magnetic field would modify the four-quark flows as well as the fixed-point structure
Fukushima and Pawlowski 2012
;
Fu and Liu 2017
. The plot in the right panel of
Figure

5
is obtained in
Fukushima and Pawlowski 2012
, which demonstrates that in the case with a magnetic field the flow pattern of the four-quark coupling has been changed and the chiral symmetry is always broken. This is in fact due to the dimensional reduction under a finite external magnetic field. Moreover, it is found that once the in-medium effects of temperature and densities are implemented, long-range correlations are screened and the vanishing critical coupling shown by the red line in the right panel of
Figure

5
is not zero anymore
Fukushima and Pawlowski 2012
.

Recently, a Fierz-complete four-fermion model is employed to investigate the phase structure at finite temperature and quark chemical potential, and it is found that the inclusion of four-quark channels other than the conventionally used scalar-pseudoscalar ones not only plays an important role in the phase diagram at large chemical potential, but also affects the dynamics at small chemical potential
Braun et al. 2017
. The related fixed-point structure is analyzed at finite chemical potential in the Fierz-complete NJL model, and it is found the dynamics is dominated by diquarks at large chemical potential
Braun et al. 2018
. Resorting to the fRG flows of four-quark interactions, one is able to observe the natural emergence of the NJL model at intermediate and low energy scales from fundamental quark-gluon interactions
Braun et al. 2020b
. Equation of state of nuclear matter at supranuclear densities is also studied in the Fierz-complete setting
Leonhardt et al. 2020
, and a maximal speed of sound is found at supranuclear densities, that is related to the emergence of color superconductivity in the regime of high densities, i.e., the formation of a diquark gap, see also
Braun and Schallmo 2022
for details. Moreover, the
U_{\mathrm{A}}(1)
symmetry and its effect on the chiral phase transition are investigated in the Fierz-complete basis
Braun et al. 2020c
.

Up to now, we have only discussed the chiral symmetry and its breaking by means of the four-fermion flows. As mentioned in the beginning of
Section

III.1
, a direct consequence of the dynamical chiral symmetry breaking is the production of mass, that is our central concern in the following. Here, we discuss a recent progress in understanding the quark mass generation and the emergence of bound states in terms of RG flows
Fu et al. 2022
. Considering only the quark degrees of freedom in
Equation

50
and extending the scalar-pseudoscalar four-quark interaction to a Fierz-complete set of
N_{f}=2
flavors, denoted by
\mathcal{B}
in the following, one arrives at a RG-scale dependent effective action, given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{x,y}\Big[Z_{q,k}(x,y)\bar{q}(x)\gamma_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}q(y)+m_{q,k}(x,y)\bar{q}(x)q(y)\Big]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{x,y,w,z}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\lambda_{\alpha,k}(x,y,w,z){\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ijlm}{\bar{q}}_{i}(x)q_{j}(y){\bar{q}}_{l}(w)q_{m}(z)\,,

(85)

with
\Phi=(q,\bar{q})
and
\int_{x,\cdots}\equiv\int d^{4}x\int\cdots
. Here
{\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ijlm}
stands for the four-quark operator of channel
\alpha
, where the indices
i
,
j
,
l
,
m
run over the Dirac, flavor and color (
N_{c}=3
) spaces, and the related coupling strength is given by
\lambda_{\alpha,k}
. In the same way, summation is assumed for repeated indices. In
Appendix

B
ten independent Fierz-complete channels of four-quark interactions of
N_{f}=2
flavors are presented.

The two-quark correlation function reads

\displaystyle\equiv-\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k,ij}(p^{\prime},p)\,,

(86)

with

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k,ij}(p^{\prime},p)\equiv-\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}}{\delta\bar{q}_{i}(p^{\prime})\delta q_{j}(p)}\bigg|_{\Phi=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[Z_{q,k}(p)i(\gamma\cdot p)_{ij}+m_{q,k}(p)\delta_{ij}\Big](2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p^{\prime}+p)\,.

(87)

Then one arrives at the quark propagator with an IR regulator, viz.,

\displaystyle G^{q\bar{q}}_{k}(p,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big[\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k}+R^{\bar{q}q}_{k}\big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=G^{q}_{k}(p)(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p^{\prime}+p)\,,

(88)

with a fermionic regulator given by

\displaystyle R^{\bar{q}q}_{k}

\displaystyle=Z_{q,k}r_{F}(p^{2}/k^{2})i\gamma\cdot p\,,

(89)

and

\displaystyle G^{q}_{k}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{q,k}(p)i\gamma\cdot p+Z_{q,k}r_{F}(p^{2}/k^{2})i\gamma\cdot p+m_{q,k}(p)}\,.

(90)

Note that the fermionic regulator in
Equation

89
, in comparison to the bosonic one in Eqs. (
9
) and (
11
), is implemented in the vector channel rather than the scalar one, which guarantees that the chiral symmetry is not broken by the regulator. In the same way, one is allowed to make a choice for the specific formalism of the fermionic regulator, e.g., the optimized one,

\displaystyle r_{F,\mathrm{opt}}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}-1\right)\Theta(1-x)\,,

(91)

or the exponential one in
Equation

10
,

\displaystyle r_{F}(x)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathrm{exp},n}(x)=\frac{x^{n-1}}{e^{x^{n}}-1}\,,

(92)

and even the simplest exponential regulator as follows

\displaystyle r_{F,\mathrm{exp}}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}e^{-x}\,.

(93)

The four-quark correlation function reads

\displaystyle\equiv-\Gamma^{(4)\bar{q}q\bar{q}q}_{k,ijlm}(p,q,r,s)\,.

(94)

with

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(4)\bar{q}q\bar{q}q}_{k,ijlm}(p,q,r,s)\equiv\frac{\delta^{4}\Gamma_{k}}{\delta\bar{q}_{i}(p)\delta q_{j}(q)\delta\bar{q}_{l}(r)\delta q_{m}(s)}\bigg|_{\Phi=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}}\Big(\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{S}(p,q,r,s)({\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ijlm}-{\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ljim})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{A}(p,q,r,s)({\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ijlm}+{\mathcal{O}}^{\alpha}_{ljim})\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p+q+r+s)\,,

(95)

where the symmetric and antisymmetric four-quark couplings read

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{S}(p,q,r,s)\equiv

\displaystyle\big(\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p,q,r,s)+\lambda_{\alpha,k}(r,q,p,s)\big)/2\,,

(96)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{A}(p,q,r,s)\equiv

\displaystyle\big(\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p,q,r,s)-\lambda_{\alpha,k}(r,q,p,s)\big)/2\,.

(97)

Figure 6:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equations for the two- and four-quark correlation functions, where prefactors and signs for each diagram are also included. The three diagrams on the r.h.s. of the flow equation of four-quark coupling stand for the
t
-,
u
-, and
s
-channels, respectively.

Neglecting the diagrams including the quark-gluon interaction in
Figure

4
and showing explicitly different channels of momenta, one is able to obtain the flow equation of four-quark coupling within the purely fermionic effective action in
Equation

85
, shown diagrammatically in the second line of
Figure

6
. We also depict the flow equation of the two-quark correlation function, i.e., the quark self-energy, in
Figure

6
.

III.1.1
Quark mass production

Figure 7:
RG flows in the plane spanned by the dimensionless quark mass
\bar{m}_{q}
and the four-quark coupling of
\sigma
-
\pi
channel
\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma\!-\!\pi}
, where logarithmic values of these two variables are used. Several evolutional trajectories with different initial conditions are labeled with numbers in circles, and arrows on trajectories point towards the IR direction. Plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2022
.

Figure 8:
Four-quark couplings
\lambda_{\alpha,k}
and their dimensionless counterparts
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k}=\lambda_{\alpha,k}k^{2}
for the ten Fierz-complete channels as functions of the RG scale
k
. Initial values of couplings are chosen as follows,
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi,k=\Lambda}=\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma,k=\Lambda}=24.2
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k=\Lambda}=0
(
\alpha\notin\{\sigma,\pi\}
) for other channels.
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}=5\times 10^{-3}
(top panels) and
1\times 10^{-2}
(bottom panels) are adopted for the initial value of quark mass. Plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2022
.

Figure 9:
Evolution of the quark mass with the RG scale obtained within the Fierz-complete basis of four-quark interactions. Initial values of couplings are chosen as follows,
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi,k=\Lambda}=\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma,k=\Lambda}=24.2
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k=\Lambda}=0
(
\alpha\notin\{\sigma,\pi\}
) for other channels. Results obtained from several different initial values of
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}
are compared. Plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2022
.

Following
Fu et al. 2022
we assume that the antisymmetric four-quark couplings
\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{A}
’s in
Equation

97
are vanishing in order to simplify calculations. Then, Eqs. (
96
) and (
97
) leaves us with

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}^{S}(p,q,r,s)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p,q,r,s)=\lambda_{\alpha,k}(r,q,p,s)\,,

(98)

and its flow equation reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\Big[\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},k}(p_{1},p_{2},q+p_{2}-p_{1},q)

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},k}(p_{3},p_{4},q,q+p_{2}-p_{1})\mathcal{F}^{t}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},k}(p_{3},p_{2},q+p_{2}-p_{3},q)

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},k}(p_{1},p_{4},q,q+p_{2}-p_{3})\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},k}(p_{1},q,p_{3},-q+p_{1}+p_{3})

\displaystyle\times\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},k}(q,p_{2},-q+p_{1}+p_{3},p_{4})\mathcal{F}^{s}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\alpha}\Big]\,,

(99)

where the superscripts
t
,
u
, and
s
of coefficient
\mathcal{F}
’s indicate that the relevant terms in
Equation

99
arise from the corresponding loop diagrams in the flow of four-quark coupling in
Figure

6
. The coefficient
\mathcal{F}
’s depend on the quark propagators and regulators, and see
Fu et al. 2022
for their explicit expressions. The flow of quark mass is readily obtained by projecting the flow equation of the quark self-energy in the first line in
Figure

6
onto the scalar channel, which reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}m_{q,k}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\big(\tilde{\partial}_{t}\bar{G}^{q}_{k}(q)\big)m_{q,k}(q)\Big[\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{\pi,k}(p,p,q,q)

\displaystyle+\frac{23}{2}\lambda_{\sigma,k}(p,p,q,q)-\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{a,k}(p,p,q,q)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\eta,k}(p,p,q,q)+\frac{8}{3}\lambda_{(S+P)_{-}^{\mathrm{adj}},k}(p,p,q,q)

\displaystyle-\frac{16}{3}\lambda_{(S+P)_{+}^{\mathrm{adj}},k}(p,p,q,q)-4\lambda_{(V+A),k}(p,p,q,q)\Big]\,,

(100)

with

\displaystyle\tilde{\partial}_{t}\bar{G}^{q}_{k}(q)

\displaystyle=-2\big(\bar{G}^{q}_{k}(q)\big)^{2}Z_{q,k}^{R}(q)q^{2}\partial_{t}R_{F,k}(q)\,.

(101)

Here one has
Z_{q,k}^{R}(q)=Z_{q,k}(q)+R_{F,k}(q)
with
R_{F,k}(q)=Z_{q,k}r_{F}(q^{2}/k^{2})
, and

\displaystyle\bar{G}^{q}_{k}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\big(Z_{q,k}^{R}(q)\big)^{2}q^{2}+m_{q,k}^{2}(q)}\,.

(102)

For the moment, we assume
Z_{q,k}=1
and use the truncation as follows

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p_{i}=0)\,,\qquad(i=1,\cdots,4)\,,

(103)

\displaystyle m_{q,k}

\displaystyle=m_{q,k}(p=0)\,,

(104)

that is, neglecting the momentum dependence of the four-quark coupling and quark mass. The dimensionless four-quark coupling
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k}=\lambda_{\alpha,k}k^{2}
and quark mass
\bar{m}_{q,k}=m_{q,k}/k
are also very useful in the following.

In order to focus on the mechanism of quark mass production, we make a further approximation as follows

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}

\displaystyle=0\,,\qquad(\alpha\notin\{\sigma,\pi\})\,,

(105)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\sigma\!-\!\pi,k}

\displaystyle\equiv\lambda_{\sigma,k}=\lambda_{\pi,k}\,,

(106)

i.e., only keeping the scalar-pseudoscalar
\sigma
and
\pi
channel. Then the flow equations in Eqs. (
99
) and (
100
) are simplified as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}=

\displaystyle 2\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}+\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}^{2}}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dx\,x^{3}{r_{F}}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\times\Big[-4\bar{m}_{q}^{2}+7x\big(1+r_{F}(x)\big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\times\frac{1+r_{F}(x)}{\Big[\big(1+r_{F}(x)\big)^{2}x+\bar{m}_{q}^{2}\Big]^{3}}\,,

(107)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{m}_{q}=

\displaystyle-\bar{m}_{q}+\bar{m}_{q}\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}\frac{13}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dx\,x^{3}{r_{F}}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\times\frac{1+r_{F}(x)}{\Big[\big(1+r_{F}(x)\big)^{2}x+\bar{m}_{q}^{2}\Big]^{2}}\,,

(108)

respectively. Here we have used the dimensionless variables, which entails that the RG scale
k
-dependence for these equations is removed. RG flows of
\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}
and
\bar{m}_{q}
in Eqs. (
107
) and (
108
) are depicted in
Figure

7
.

The plane in
Figure

7
is segmented into two parts by the red solid line. In the chiral limit the red line crosses the
x
-axis at the UV fixed point, as shown in
Figure

5
, and the critical value here is
\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma-\pi}^{c}=23.08
. Interestingly, the UV critical point is extended to being an approximate critical line in the flow diagram in
Figure

7
, where the word “approximate” is used because the exact chiral symmetry is lost once the quark mass is nonzero. On the l.h.s. of the critical line, there is little dynamical chiral symmetry breaking and the quark mass is dominated by the current mass, while on the r.h.s. the dynamical chiral symmetry breaking plays a dominant role. Furthermore, it is observed that in the regime of dynamical chiral symmetry breaking, that is, on the r.h.s. of the red line, the dimensionless four-quark coupling increases firstly and then decreases. This is due to the competition between the flow equations of the quark self-energy and the four-quark coupling shown in
Figure

6
, where the fish diagrams drive the dynamical breaking of chiral symmetry and result in the increase of the quark mass via the flow of quark self-energy, and in turn the increase of quark mass suppresses fluctuations of the four-quark flow. Finally, a balance is obtained with the decrease of RG scale, where the dimensional
m_{q,k}
and
\lambda_{\sigma-\pi,k}
are not dependent on
k
any more.

In
Figure

8
the evolution of four-quark couplings
\lambda_{\alpha,k}
and their dimensionless counterparts
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k}=\lambda_{\alpha,k}k^{2}
with the RG scale for ten Fierz-complete channels is shown. The results are obtained from calculations, in which the initial values of couplings are chosen to be
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi,k=\Lambda}=\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma,k=\Lambda}=24.2
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k=\Lambda}=0
(
\alpha\notin\{\sigma,\pi\}
) for other channels, i.e., the coupling strength of channels except the
\sigma
and
\pi
ones is assumed to be vanishing at the UV cutoff. In the meantime, results obtained from two initial values of quark mass, viz.,
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}=5\times 10^{-3}
(top panels) and
1\times 10^{-2}
(bottom panels), are compared. In both cases one finds that the
\pi
and
\sigma
channels play a dominant role in the whole range of RG scale, and they are no longer degenerate with the scale evolving towards the IR limit. The strength of the
\pi
channel is larger than that of the
\sigma
channel. Moreover, one observes that the interaction strength of channels
(V-A)^{\mathrm{adj}}
in
Equation

415
and
(S+P)^{\mathrm{adj}}_{-}
in
Equation

417
are also excited to some values, though they are significantly smaller than those of the
\pi
and
\sigma
channels. On the contrary, magnitudes of the remaining channels are very small, and they could be neglected in the whole range of RG scale. In
Figure

9
dependence of the quark mass on the RG scale is shown, and in the same way calculations are done with the Fierz-complete basis of four-quark interactions. Same initial values of the four-quark couplings as those in
Figure

8
are employed, and results obtained from different initial values of the quark mass are compared.

III.1.2
Natural emergence of bound states

Figure 10:
Schematic diagram showing emergence of a resonance at the pole of relevant meson mass from the four-quark vertex, where the square and half-circles stand for the full four-quark and quark-meson vertices, respectively. The dashed line denotes the meson propagator.

Figure 11:
Left panel: Dependence of
1/\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
, i.e., the inverse four-quark coupling of the pion channel, on the Mandelstam variable
t=P^{2}=P_{0}^{2}+\vec{P}^{2}
with
\vec{P}=0
, where a
3d
regulator is used. Data points stand for results computed directly from the analytic flow equation in
Equation

116
both in the Euclidean (
P_{0}^{2}>0
) and Minkowski (
P_{0}^{2}<0
) regimes. The solid and dashed lines show results of analytic continuation from
P_{0}^{2}>0
to
P_{0}^{2}<0
based on the Padé approximation and the fitting function in
Equation

123
, respectively. Several different values of the quark mass at the UV cutoff
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}
are adopted, while the initial values of four-quark couplings are fixed with
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi,k=\Lambda}=\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma,k=\Lambda}=16.92
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k=\Lambda}=0
(
\alpha\notin\{\sigma,\pi\}
). Right panel: Pion mass as a function of the quark mass at the UV cutoff
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}
, where the flow equation of four-quark coupling in
Equation

116
is solved directly in the Minkowski spacetime with a
3d
regulator. Here same initial values of four-quark couplings as those in the left panel are used. The several values of the pion mass extracted in the left panel are also shown on the curve in scattering points. Plots are adopted from
Fu et al. 2022
.

Properties of bound states of quarks or antiquarks, e.g., the pions and nucleons, in principle can be inferred from the relevant four-point and six-point vertices of quarks, in some specific channels and regimes of momenta
Eichmann et al. 2016
. In
Figure

10
a sketch map shows how this happens at the example of mesons. The square denotes a four-point vertex of quark and antiquark. If the total momentum of a quark and an antiquark, denoted by
P
here, is in the Minkowski spacetime and in the vicinity of the on-shell pole mass of a meson in some channel, i.e.,
P^{2}\sim-m_{\mathrm{meson}}^{2}
, the full four-quark vertex can be well described by a resonance of the meson, as shown on the r.h.s. of
Figure

10
, where two quark-meson vertices are connected with the propagator of meson. Therefore, one has to calculate the full four-quark vertex or quark-meson vertex in some specific regime of momentum, which is usually realized by resuming a four-quark kernel to the order of infinity in the formalism of Bethe-Salpeter equations
Salpeter and Bethe 1951
;
Nakanishi 1969
. Note that the necessary resummation for the four-quark vertex is well included in the flow equation of four-quark couplings in
Equation

99
, and it is, therefore, natural to expect that the RG flows are also well-suited for the description of bound states as same as the quark mass production in
Section

III.1.1
. Moreover, the advantage of RG flows is evident, that is, the self-consistency between
the bound states encoded in the flow of four-quark vertices in
Equation

99
and that of quark mass gap in
Equation

100
can be well guaranteed, once a truncation is made on the level of the effective action, such as that in
Equation

85
.

In order to investigate the resonance behavior of four-quark vertices in
Equation

99
, one has to go beyond the truncation in
Equation

103
and include appropriate momentum dependence for the four-quark vertices. The external momenta of couplings in
Equation

99
are parameterized as follows

\displaystyle p_{1}=

\displaystyle p+\frac{P}{2}\,,\qquad p_{2}=p-\frac{P}{2}\,,

(109)

\displaystyle p_{3}=

\displaystyle p^{\prime}-\frac{P}{2}\,,\qquad p_{4}=p^{\prime}+\frac{P}{2}\,.

(110)

Then, one is left with the relevant Mandelstam variables given by

\displaystyle s=

\displaystyle(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}=(p+p^{\prime})^{2}\,,

(111)

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}=P^{2}\,,

(112)

\displaystyle u=

\displaystyle(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2}=(p-p^{\prime})^{2}\,.

(113)

In the following, we focus on the
\pi
meson and assume, that the total momentum of quark and antiquark in the
t
-channel is near the regime of on-shell pion mass, viz., one has the
t
-variable
P^{2}\sim-m_{\pi}^{2}
in
Equation

112
. Consequently, the four-quark coupling of the pion channel would be significantly larger than those of other channels, and its dependence on external momenta would be dominated by the
t
-variable. Thus, one is allowed to make the approximation as follows

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi,k}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle\simeq\lambda_{\pi,k}(P^{2})\,,

(114)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha,k}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle\simeq\lambda_{\alpha,k}(0)\,,\qquad\alpha\neq\pi.

(115)

Furthermore, insofar as the four-quark vertices on the r.h.s. of the flow of coupling in the second line of
Figure

6
, a simple analysis of relevant momenta for each vertex indicates, that the
t
-variable dependence is only required to be kept for the vertices in the diagram of
t
channel, i.e., the first diagram. One is thus
allowed to simplify the flow equation of
\lambda_{\pi,k}
in
Equation

99
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\lambda_{\pi,k}(P^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{k}(P^{2})\lambda_{\pi,k}^{2}(P^{2})+\mathcal{A}_{k}(t,u,s)\,,

(116)

with two coefficients given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}(P^{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\mathcal{F}^{t}_{\pi\pi,\pi}\,,

(117)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k}(t,u,s)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\bigg\{\sum_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}}\Big[\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime},k}\lambda_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},k}\big(\mathcal{F}^{t}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\pi}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\pi}+\mathcal{F}^{s}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime},\pi}\big)\Big]-\lambda_{\pi,k}^{2}\mathcal{F}^{t}_{\pi\pi,\pi}\bigg\}\,.

(118)

Note that all the four-quark couplings in
Equation

118
are momentum independent. If one adopts further
p=p^{\prime}=0
in Eqs. (
109
) and (
110
), two Mandelstam variables are vanishing, i.e.,
s=u=0
, and one arrives at

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k}(t,u,s)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{k}(P^{2})\,.

(119)

One is able to observe the natural emergence of a bound state arising from resummation of the four-quark vertex from
Equation

116
, whose solution is readily obtained once the last term on the r.h.s. is ignored. One has

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi,k=0}(P^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{\pi,k=\Lambda}}{1-\lambda_{\pi,k=\Lambda}\int^{0}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{k}(P^{2})\frac{dk}{k}}\,,

(120)

with
\lambda_{\pi,k=\Lambda}
the four-quark coupling strength of the pion channel at the UV cutoff, that is independent of external momenta. Evidently, when a value of
P^{2}
is chosen appropriately, such that the denominator in
Equation

120
is vanishing, the four-quark coupling in the IR
\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
is divergent. As a consequence, one can employ this condition to determine the pole mass of the bound state, i.e., the pion mass, which reads

\displaystyle 1-\lambda_{\pi,k=\Lambda}\int^{0}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{C}_{k}(P^{2}=-m_{\pi}^{2})\frac{dk}{k}=0\,.

(121)

When the coefficient
\mathcal{A}_{k}(P^{2})
in
Equation

116
is taken into account, there is no analytic solution anymore. However, as would be shown in the following, direct numerical calculation of
Equation

116
indicates that the qualitative behavior of pole displayed by
Equation

120
is not changed.

In the left panel of
Figure

11
the inverse four-quark coupling of the pion channel in the IR limit, i.e.,
1/\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
, is shown as a function of the Mandelstam variable
t=P^{2}=P_{0}^{2}+\vec{P}^{2}
with
\vec{P}=0
. In order to solve the flow equation of four-quark coupling in
Equation

116
directly in the Minkowski spacetime with
P^{2}<0
, one has employed the
3d
regulator as follows

\displaystyle R^{\bar{q}q}_{k}

\displaystyle=Z_{q,k}r_{F}({\vec{p}}^{2}/k^{2})i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}\,,

(122)

in lieu of the
4d
one in
Equation

89
. Relevant results are shown in the plot by scattering points, where different symbols correspond to several different values of the quark mass at the UV cutoff scale
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}
. The
3d
regulator allows one to compute the flow equation of the four-quark coupling not only in the Euclidean (
P^{2}>0
) but also Minkowski (
P^{2}<0
) regimes, viz., the part on the r.h.s. of the dashed vertical line in the plot and that on the l.h.s., respectively. As shown in Eqs. (
120
) and (
121
), the pole mass of pion is determined from position of the zero point of
1/\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
, that is, the crossing point between the horizontal dashed line and those of
1/\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
in the left panel of
Figure

11
. Besides the direct calculations, results of analytic continuation from the Euclidean to Minkowski regimes are also presented in the left panel of
Figure

11
. Two methods of analytic continuation are employed. One is to fit a simple function which reads

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi,k=0}(P^{2})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{a_{0}+a_{2}P^{2}+a_{4}P^{4}}{c_{0}+P^{2}+c_{4}P^{4}}\,.

(123)

Here the coefficients
a_{0}
,
a_{2}
,
a_{4}
,
c_{0}
, and
c_{4}
are determined by fitting the numerical results of
P_{0}^{2}>0
from
Equation

116
, and then results of
P_{0}^{2}<0
are predicted by
Equation

123
. The other is the Padé approximation, where the simple function on the r.h.s. of
Equation

123
is replaced by a Padé fraction, to wit,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pi,k=0}[n,n](P^{2})

\displaystyle\approx\lambda_{\pi,k=0}(P^{2})\,,\qquad P^{2}>0\,.

(124)

Here, a diagonal fraction with the same order of the polynomials
n
in the numerator and denominator is used. Note that the simple function in
Equation

123
is in fact a Padé fraction of order
n=2
. The order of Padé fraction is varied with
n=25\sim 100
in the calculations. In the left panel of
Figure

11
the analytically continued results based on the two methods are in comparison to those of direct computation in the Minkowski region with
P^{2}<0
. Remarkably, it is found that both the analytically continued results are in excellent agreement with the data points obtained from
Equation

116
. Moreover, in order to verify Goldstone theorem and the nature of Goldstone boson of the pion in the RG flow, one shows the extracted pion mass as a function of the quark mass at the UV cutoff in the right panel of
Figure

11
. Evidently, the pion mass is found to decrease with the decreasing current quark mass, and it is exactly massless in the chiral limit.

Figure 12:

1/\lambda_{\pi,k=0}
, i.e., the inverse four-quark coupling of the
\pi
channel, on the Mandelstam variable
t=P^{2}=P_{0}^{2}+\vec{P}^{2}
with
\vec{P}=0
, obtained with the
4d
regulator. Data points stand for the results calculated in the flow equation in the Euclidean (
P_{0}^{2}>0
) region. The solid and dashed lines show analytically continued results from
P_{0}^{2}>0
to
P_{0}^{2}<0
based on the Padé approximation and the fitting function in

, respectively. Several different values of the quark mass at the UV cutoff
\bar{m}_{q,k=\Lambda}
are adopted, while the initial values of four-quark couplings are fixed with
\bar{\lambda}_{\pi,k=\Lambda}=\bar{\lambda}_{\sigma,k=\Lambda}=22.55
and
\bar{\lambda}_{\alpha,k=\Lambda}=0
(

). The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2022
.

) for different values of the quark mass at the UV cutoff

, where a

and the Padé approximation in

, respectively. The initial values of four-quark couplings are fixed with

). The table is adopted from
