.

In
Figure

12
one shows the same physical quantities as the left panel of
Figure

11
, but obtained with the
4d
regulator. Quite apparently, in the
4d
case direct calculation of the flow equation in
Equation

116
is only accessible in the Euclidean region, as shown in the scattering points in
Figure

12
. Thus one has to rely on the analytic continuation to infer the pole mass of pion, and the relevant results are also presented in the plot, where the same two methods of analytic continuation as the case of
3d
regulator is used. It is found that, in comparison to the analytically continued results of the
3d
regulator, those of the
4d
case have significant larger errors, as shown by the bands in
Figure

12
. The errors are inferred from varying the range of
P_{0}
, i.e.,
(0,0.1\Lambda)
,
(0,0.2\Lambda)
,
(0,0.3\Lambda)
, that is used to fix the analytically continued functions in
Equation

123
or
Equation

124
. In
Table

1
one shows the analytically continued values of the pole mass of pion.

III.2
Quark-meson model

In this section we discuss another formalism of the low energy effective field theories, i.e., the quark-meson model, which in principle can be obtained from the NJL model via the bosonization with the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation. The effective interactions between quarks and mesons in the rebosonized QCD effective action in
Equation

50
, and their natural emergence resulting from the RG evolution of fundamental degrees of freedom will be discussed in detail in
Section

IV.4
. There is a wealth of studies in the literature relevant to the QM model within the fRG approach, see, e.g.,
Papp et al. 2000
;
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
;
Schaefer and Wambach 2007
;
Schaefer and Wambach 2008
;
Skokov et al. 2010
;
Herbst et al. 2011
;
Skokov et al. 2010
;
Skokov et al. 2011
;
Braun et al. 2012
;
Strodthoff et al. 2012
;
Schaefer and Wagner 2012
;
Aoki and Sato 2013
;
Kamikado et al. 2013
;
Jiang and Zhuang 2012
;
Haas et al. 2013
;
Mitter and Schaefer 2014
;
Herbst et al. 2014
;
Herbst et al. 2013
;
Tripolt et al. 2014a
;
Tripolt et al. 2014b
;
Grahl and Rischke 2013
;
Morita et al. 2013
;
Pawlowski and Rennecke 2014
;
Helmboldt et al. 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2016
;
Khan et al. 2015
;
Wang and Zhuang 2016
;
Mueller and Pawlowski 2015
;
Eser et al. 2015
;
Weyrich et al. 2015
;
Fejos 2015
;
Jiang et al. 2016
;
Fu et al. 2016
;
Rennecke and Schaefer 2017
;
Jung et al. 2017
;
Jung et al. 2017
;
Fejos and Hosaka 2016
;
Almasi et al. 2017a
;
Posfay et al. 2018
;
Yokota et al. 2016
;
Resch et al. 2019
;
Tripolt et al. 2018
;
Fejos and Hosaka 2017
;
Yokota et al. 2017
;
Almasi et al. 2017b
;
Zhang et al. 2017
;
Fu et al. 2020b
;
Fu et al. 2019
;
Sun et al. 2018
;
Wen et al. 2019
;
Fejos and Hosaka 2018
;
Braun et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2019a
;
Wen and Fu 2021
;
Yin et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2019a
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Fu et al. 2021
;
Chen et al. 2021
.

For the moment, we only consider the degrees of freedom of quarks and mesons and their interactions via the Yukawa coupling in
Equation

50
. The
N_{f}=2
flavor QM effective action reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}=

\displaystyle\int_{x}\bigg\{Z_{q,k}\bar{q}\Big[\gamma_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\gamma_{0}(\hat{\mu}+igA_{0})\Big]q+\frac{1}{2}Z_{\phi,k}(\partial_{\mu}\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle+h_{k}\bar{q}\big(T^{0}\sigma+i\gamma_{5}\bm{T}\cdot\bm{\pi}\big)q+V_{k}(\rho,A_{0})-c\sigma\bigg\}\,,

(125)

Note that explanations for most notations in
Equation

125
can be found in
Section

II.3
below
Equation

50
. The effective potential in
Equation

125
can be decomposed into a sum of the contribution from the glue sector and that from the matter sector, which corresponds to the first and last two loops of the flow in
Figure

2
, respectively. Thus, one is led to

\displaystyle V_{k}(\rho,A_{0})=

\displaystyle V_{\mathrm{glue},k}(A_{0})+V_{\mathrm{mat},k}(\rho,A_{0})\,,

(126)

where the first term on the r.h.s. is the glue potential or the Polyakov loop potential, since it is usually reformulated by means of the Polyakov loop
L(A_{0})
, and the latter can be calculated through the flow equation within the QM model. In the following, we still use
V_{k}(\rho)
to stand for
V_{\mathrm{mat},k}
for simplicity in a slight abuse of notation.

III.2.1
Flow of the effective potential

The flow equation of the effective potential reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{k}(\rho)=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{4}}{4\pi^{2}}\bigg[\big(N^{2}_{f}-1\big)l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle+l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\sigma,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle-4N_{c}N_{f}l^{(F,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k},\eta_{q,k};T,\mu)\bigg]\,,

(127)

with the threshold functions given by

\displaystyle l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{\phi,k}}{5}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k}}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+n_{B}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k};T)\right)\,,

(128)

and

\displaystyle l_{0}^{(F,4)}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k},\eta_{q,k};T,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{q,k}}{4}\right)\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\times\Big(1-n_{F}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k};T,\mu,L,\bar{L})-n_{F}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k};T,-\mu,\bar{L},L)\Big)\,,

(129)

where the bosonic distribution function reads

\displaystyle n_{B}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\exp\bigg\{\frac{k}{T}\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k}}\bigg\}-1}\,,

(130)

and the fermionic one

\displaystyle n_{F}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k};T,\mu,L,\bar{L})

\displaystyle=\frac{1+2\bar{L}e^{x/T}+Le^{2x/T}}{1+3\bar{L}e^{x/T}+3Le^{2x/T}+e^{3x/T}}\,,

(131)

with
x=k\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k}}-\mu
. Here,
L
and
\bar{L}
are the Polyakov loop and its conjugate. The dimensionless, renormalized meson and quark masses squared in
Equation

127
read

\displaystyle\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{V^{\prime}_{k}(\rho)}{k^{2}Z_{\phi,k}}\,,\qquad\tilde{m}^{2}_{\sigma,k}=\frac{V^{\prime}_{k}(\rho)+2\rho V^{\prime\prime}_{k}(\rho)}{k^{2}Z_{\phi,k}}\,,

(132)

\displaystyle\tilde{m}^{2}_{q,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{h^{2}_{k}\rho}{2k^{2}Z^{2}_{q,k}}\,.

(133)

The quark and meson anomalous dimensions in
Equation

127
are given by

\displaystyle\eta_{q,k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial_{t}Z_{q,k}}{Z_{q,k}}\,,\qquad\eta_{\phi,k}=-\frac{\partial_{t}Z_{\phi,k}}{Z_{\phi,k}}\,,

(134)

computation of which can be found in
Section

IV.1
.

There are two classes of methods to solve the flow equation of the effective potential in
Equation

127
. The methods of the first class capture local properties of the potential and are very convenient for numerical calculations, but fail to obtain global properties of the potential. A typical method in this class is the Taylor expansion of the effective potential. On the contrary, the methods in the other class are able to provide us with the global properties of the potential, but usually their numerical implements are relatively more difficult. The second class includes the discretization of the potential on a grid
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
, the pseudo-spectral methods
Boyd 2000
;
Borchardt and Knorr 2015
;
Borchardt and Knorr 2016
;
Knorr 2021
, e.g., the Chebyshev expansion of the potential
Chen et al. 2021
, the discontinuous Galerkin method
Grossi and Wink 2019
;
Grossi et al. 2021
, etc. In what follows we give a brief discussion about the Taylor expansion and the Chebyshev expansion of the potential.

The Taylor expansion of the effective potential reads

\displaystyle V_{k}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{v}}\frac{\lambda_{n,k}}{n!}(\rho-\kappa_{k})^{n}\,,

(135)

with the expansion coefficients
\lambda_{n,k}
and the expansion point
\kappa_{k}
that might be
k
-dependent, where
N_{v}
is the maximal expanding order in the calculations. Reformulating
Equation

135
in terms of the renormalized variables, one is led to

\displaystyle\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{v}}\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{n,k}}{n!}(\bar{\rho}-\bar{\kappa}_{k})^{n}\,,

(136)

with

\displaystyle\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})

\displaystyle=V_{\mathrm{mat},k}(\rho)\,,\qquad\;\bar{\rho}=Z_{\phi,k}\rho\,,

(137)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{k}

\displaystyle=Z_{\phi,k}\kappa_{k}\,,\qquad\bar{\lambda}_{n,k}=\frac{\lambda_{n,k}}{(Z_{\phi,k})^{n}}\,,

(138)

Substituting
Equation

136
into the left hand side of
Equation

127
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial^{n}_{\bar{\rho}}\left(\partial_{t}\big|_{\rho}\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})\right)\Big|_{\bar{\rho}=\bar{\kappa}_{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}-n\eta_{\phi,k})\bar{\lambda}_{n,k}-(\partial_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{k}+\eta_{\phi,k}\bar{\kappa}_{k})\bar{\lambda}_{n+1,k}\,.

(139)

If the expansion point
\kappa_{k}
is independent of
k
, i.e.,
\partial_{t}\kappa_{k}=0
, which is usually called as the fixed point expansion, the expression in the second bracket on the r.h.s. of
Equation

139
is vanishing. One can see that in this case the expansion coefficients of different orders in
Equation

136
decouples from each other in the flow equations, which results in an improved convergence for the Taylor expansion and a better numerical stability
Pawlowski and Rennecke 2014
. Another commonly used choice is the running expansion, whereof one expands the potential around the field on the EoS for every value of
k
, that is,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\rho}}\Big(\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})-\bar{c}_{k}\bar{\sigma}\Big)\bigg|_{\bar{\rho}=\bar{\kappa}_{k}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(140)

with

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}

\displaystyle=Z_{\phi,k}^{1/2}\sigma\,,\qquad\bar{c}_{k}=Z_{\phi,k}^{-1/2}c\,.

(141)

Here
c
is independent of
k
. Then one arrives at the flow of the expansion point, as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{\kappa}_{k}=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{\bar{c}_{k}^{2}}{\bar{\lambda}_{1,k}^{3}+\bar{c}_{k}^{2}\bar{\lambda}_{2,k}}\Bigg[\partial_{\bar{\rho}}\left(\partial_{t}\big|_{\rho}\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})\right)\Big|_{\bar{\rho}=\bar{\kappa}_{k}}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt+\eta_{\phi,k}\left(\frac{\bar{\lambda}_{1,k}}{2}+\bar{\kappa}_{k}\bar{\lambda}_{2,k}\right)\Bigg]\,.

(142)

For more discussions about the fixed and running expansions, see, e.g.,
Pawlowski and Rennecke 2014
;
Braun et al. 2016a
;
Rennecke 2015b
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
;
Rennecke and Schaefer 2017
;
Yin et al. 2019
. In the same way, the field dependence of the Yukawa coupling, i.e.,
h_{k}(\rho)
can also be studied within a similar Taylor expansion
Pawlowski and Rennecke 2014
;
Yin et al. 2019
, which encodes higher-order quark-meson interactions. Moreover, effects of the thermal splitting for the mesonic wave function renormalization in the heat bath are also investigated in
Yin et al. 2019
.

As for the Chebyshev expansion, the effective potential reads

\displaystyle\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})

\displaystyle=\sum^{N_{v}}_{n=1}c_{n,k}T_{n}(\bar{\rho})+\frac{1}{2}c_{0,k}\,,

(143)

with the Chebyshev polynomials
T_{n}(\bar{\rho})
and the respective expansion coefficients
c_{n,k}
. Then, one arrives at the flow of the effective potential as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big|_{\rho}\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho})=

\displaystyle\sum^{N_{v}}_{n=1}\Big(\partial_{t}c_{n,k}-d_{n,k}\eta_{\phi,k}(\bar{\rho})\bar{\rho}\Big)T_{n}(\bar{\rho})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\partial_{t}c_{0,k}-d_{0,k}\eta_{\phi,k}(\bar{\rho})\bar{\rho}\Big)\,,

(144)

where the field dependence of the meson wave function renormalization
Z_{\phi,k}(\rho)
as well as the meson anomalous dimension
\eta_{\phi,k}(\bar{\rho})
is taken into account. Note that the coefficients
d_{n,k}
are related to
c_{n,k}
through a recursion relation as shown in
Chen et al. 2021
. The flow equations of the coefficients in
Equation

143
are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{m,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{N+1}\sum^{N}_{i=0}\Big(\partial_{t}\big|_{\rho}\bar{V}_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{i})\Big)T_{m}(\bar{\rho}_{i})

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{N+1}\sum^{N_{v}}_{n=1}\sum^{N}_{i=0}d_{n,k}T_{m}(\bar{\rho}_{i})T_{n}(\bar{\rho}_{i})\eta_{\phi,k}(\bar{\rho}_{i})\bar{\rho}_{i}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N+1}d_{0,k}\sum^{N}_{i=0}T_{m}(\bar{\rho}_{i})\eta_{\phi,k}(\bar{\rho}_{i})\bar{\rho}_{i}\,,

(145)

where the summation for
i
is performed on
N+1
zeros of the polynomial
T_{N+1}(\bar{\rho})
.

III.2.2
Quark-meson model of
N_{f}=2+1
flavors

The effective action for the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model, see, e.g.,
Mitter and Schaefer 2014
;
Herbst et al. 2014
;
Rennecke and Schaefer 2017
;
Fu et al. 2020b
;
Wen et al. 2019
;
Wen and Fu 2021
, reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}=

\displaystyle\int_{x}\bigg\{Z_{q,k}\bar{q}\Big[\gamma_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\gamma_{0}(\hat{\mu}+igA_{0})\Big]q+h_{k}\,\bar{q}\,\Sigma_{5}q

\displaystyle+Z_{\phi,k}\text{tr}(\bar{D}_{\mu}\Sigma\cdot\bar{D}_{\mu}\Sigma^{\dagger})+V_{\text{\tiny{glue}}}(L,\bar{L})+V_{k}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle-c_{A}\xi-c_{l}\sigma_{l}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,c_{s}\sigma_{s}\bigg\}\,,

(146)

where the scalar and pseudoscalar mesonic fields of nonets are in the adjoint representation of the
U(N_{f}=3)
group, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=T^{a}(\sigma^{a}+i\pi^{a})\,,\quad a=0,\,1,...,8\,.

(147)

with
T^{0}=1/\sqrt{2N_{f}}\mathbb{1}_{N_{f}\times N_{f}}
and
T^{a}=\lambda^{a}/2
for
a=1,...,8
. Here
\lambda^{a}
are the Gell-Mann matrices. The covariant derivative on the mesonic fields is given by

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{\mu}\Sigma

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}+\delta_{\mu 0}\big[\hat{\mu},\Sigma\big]\,.

(148)

where
\big[\hat{\mu},\Sigma\big]
denotes the commutator between the matrix of chemical potentials and the meson matrix in
Equation

147
. Note that although mesons do not carry the baryon chemical potential, they might have chemical potentials for the electric charge and the strangeness. The quark chemical potentials are related to those of conserved charges through the relations as follows

\displaystyle\mu_{u}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\mu_{B}+\frac{2}{3}\mu_{Q}\,,

(149)

\displaystyle\mu_{d}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\mu_{B}-\frac{1}{3}\mu_{Q}\,,

(150)

\displaystyle\mu_{s}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\mu_{B}-\frac{1}{3}\mu_{Q}-\mu_{S}\,.

(151)

For the Yukawa coupling, one has

\displaystyle\Sigma_{5}=

\displaystyle T^{a}(\sigma^{a}+i\gamma_{5}\pi^{a})\,.

(152)

In contradistinction to the two-flavor case, the effective potential in
Equation

146
is a function of two chiral invariants, to wit,

\displaystyle\rho_{1}

\displaystyle=\text{tr}(\Sigma\cdot\Sigma^{\dagger})\,,

(153)

\displaystyle\rho_{2}

\displaystyle=\text{tr}\Big(\Sigma\cdot\Sigma^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{3}\,\rho_{1}\,\mathbb{1}_{3\times 3}\Big)^{2}\,.

(154)

which are both invariant not only under
SU_{\text{A}}(3)
but also
U_{\text{A}}(1)
. On the EoM these two invariants read

\displaystyle\rho_{1}\big|_{\mathrm{EoM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{l}^{2}+\sigma_{s}^{2})\,,

(155)

\displaystyle\rho_{2}\big|_{\mathrm{EoM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}(\sigma_{l}^{2}-2\sigma_{s}^{2})^{2}\,,

(156)

where the light and strange fields are related to the zeroth and eighth components in
Equation

147
through the relation as follows

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{l}\\
\phi_{s}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\sqrt{2}\\
-\sqrt{2}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{8}\\
\phi_{0}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(157)

The term of Kobayashi-Maskawa-’t Hooft determinant in
Equation

146
preserves
SU_{\text{A}}(3)
while breaks
U_{\text{A}}(1)
with

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\det(\Sigma)+\det(\Sigma^{\dagger})\,,

(158)

and the breaking strength is described by the coefficient
c_{A}
. The light and strange quark masses read

\displaystyle m_{l,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{k}}{2}\sigma_{l},\qquad m_{s,k}=\frac{h_{k}}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{s}\,,

(159)

and the pion and kaon decay constants are given by

\displaystyle f_{\pi}=\sigma_{l},\quad f_{K}=\frac{\sigma_{l}+\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{s}}{2}\,,

(160)

where
\sigma_{l}
and
\sigma_{s}
are on their respective equations of motion. For more discussions about the flow equations in the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model, see the aforementioned references in this section.

III.2.3
Phase structure

Figure 13:
Phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2
flavor QM model in the chiral limit in the plane of the temperature and quark chemical potential obtained with discretization of the potential on a grid in
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
. The plot is adopted from
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
.

Figure 14:
Phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2
flavor QM model in the
T-\mu_{B}
plane obtained with the Chebyshev expansion of the potential in
Chen et al. 2021
. The plot is adopted from
Chen et al. 2021
.

Figure 15:
Phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2
flavor QM model in the
T-\mu_{q}
plane obtained with a discontinuous Galerkin method in
Grossi et al. 2021
, where the color stands for the value of the order parameter. The plot is adopted from
Grossi et al. 2021
.

Figure 16:
Entropy density for the 3
d
flat regulator (left panel) and 3
d
mass-like regulator (right panel) close to the zero temperature chiral phase boundary obtained in the QM model in LPA
Otto et al. 2022
, where the solid and dashed lines denote the first-order phase transition and the crossover, respectively, and the black dot stands for the CEP. The blue region shows a negative entropy density. The plots are adopted from
Otto et al. 2022
.

Figure 17:
Phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model in the
T-\mu_{B}
plane obtained in
Fu et al. 2020b
, where the phase boundaries in the regime of crossover for the two cases:
\mu_{S}=0
and the strangeness neutrality
n_{S}=0
are compared. The color stands for the relative error of the reduced condensate (see
Section

IV.5
) for the two cases. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020b
.

In
Figure

13
the phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2
flavor QM model in the chiral limit obtained in
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
is shown. In order to investigate the phase structure, especially in the regime of large chemical potential, the global information of the effective potential in
Equation

127
is indispensable. Thus, the potential is discretized on a grid to resolve the flow equation, and see
Schaefer and Wambach 2005
for more details. One can see that in the region of small chemical potentials, there is a second-order chiral phase transition denoted by the blue dashed line. With the decrease of the temperature, the second-order phase transition is changed into the first-order one at the tricritical point denoted by a green asterisk. The red solid lines stand for the first-order phase transition. Moreover, with the further decrease of the temperature, one observes that the first-order phase transition splits into two phase transition lines, one of which even evolves again into the second-order phase transition at high chemical potentials. Note that the first-order phase transition line backbends at high chemical potentials
Tripolt et al. 2018
.

In
Figure

14
the phase diagram of the
N_{f}=2
flavor QM model obtained with the Chebyshev expansion in
Chen et al. 2021
is shown. The black dashed line denotes the
O(4)
second-order phase transition in the chiral limit, and the black dot stands for the tricritical point. The back solid line is the first-order phase transition in the chiral limit, where the splitting at large baryon chemical potential as shown in
Figure

13
is not shown explicitly here, and only the left branch is depicted. The solid lines of different colors in
Figure

14
correspond to different strengths of the explicit chiral symmetry breaking, i.e., different pion masses, and their end points, that is, the critical end points (CEP) comprise a
Z(2)
second-order phase transition line, which is denoted by the red dashed line.

As we have mentioned above, the first-order phase transition line backbends at large chemical potential. It is conjectured that this artifact might arise from the defect that the discontinuity for the potential at high baryon chemical potential is not well dealt with within the grid or pseudo-spectral methods. Recently, a discontinuous Galerkin method has been developed to resolve the flow equation of the effective potential
Grossi and Wink 2019
;
Grossi et al. 2021
, and the relevant result for the two-flavor phase diagram is shown in
Figure

15

Grossi et al. 2021
. Within this approach, the formation and propagation of shocks are allowed, and see
Grossi and Wink 2019
;
Grossi et al. 2021
for a detailed discussion.

However, very recently another research in
Otto et al. 2022
finds that the back-bending behavior of the chiral phase boundary at large chemical potential has another different origin. It is found that the appearance of the back-bending behavior depends on the employed regulator
Otto et al. 2022
. In
Figure

16
results of the chiral phase boundary and the entropy density obtained with two different regulators are compared. The left panel corresponds to the usually used 3
d
flat or optimized regulator, cf.
Equation

12
and
Equation

91
, and the right is obtained with a 3
d
mass-like regulator, see
Otto et al. 2022
for more details. The same truncation, i.e., the LPA approximation, is used in both calculations. It is observed that the usually used flat (Litim) regulator results in a back-bending of the chiral phase line at large chemical potential, which is also accompanied by a region of negative entropy density in the chirally symmetric phase, as shown by the blue area in the left panel of
Figure

16
. On the contrary, in the right panel one finds that the chiral phase line shows no back-bending behavior and the entropy density stays positive if a mass-like regulator is used. See
Otto et al. 2022
for a more detailed discussion.

In heavy-ion collisions since the incident nuclei do not carry the strangeness, the produced QCD matter after collisions is of strangeness neutrality, that is, the strangeness density
n_{S}
is vanishing. Usually, the condition of strangeness neutrality requires that the chemical potential of strangeness
\mu_{S}
in
Equation

151
is nonvanishing with
\mu_{B}\neq 0

Fu et al. 2020b
;
Fu et al. 2019
. The influence of the strangeness neutrality on the phase boundary is investigated in
Fu et al. 2020b
, which is presented in
Figure

17
. One can see in comparison to the case of
\mu_{S}=0
, the phase boundary moves up slightly due to the strangeness neutrality. In another words, the curvature of the phase boundary is decreased a bit by the condition of strangeness neutrality, and see
Section

IV.6
for more discussions about the curvature of phase boundary.

III.2.4
Equation of state

Figure 18:
Reduced chiral condensate as a function of the reduced temperature in the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model obtained in
Herbst et al. 2014
. The fRG result (red solid line) is compared with the lattice result
Borsanyi et al. 2010a
as well as the mean-field ones. The plot is adopted from
Herbst et al. 2014
.

Figure 19:
Pressure (left panel) and trace anomaly (right panel) as functions of the reduced temperature in the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model obtained in
Herbst et al. 2014
. The fRG results are in comparison to the lattice
Bazavov 2013
;
Borsanyi et al. 2010b
and mean-field results. The plots are adopted from
Herbst et al. 2014
.

Figure 20:
Left panel: Trace anomaly of the
N_{f}=2
QM model as a function of the temperature. Here the LPA result is compared with that beyond LPA, denoted by “full”, in which the wave function renormalizations for the mesons and quarks, the RG scale dependence of the Yukawa coupling are taken into account. Right panel: Trace anomaly of the
N_{f}=2+1
QM model obtained beyond LPA (labelled with“LPA after rescale”) as a function of the temperature, in comparison to the LPA result
Herbst et al. 2014
and the lattice result from Wuppertal-Budapest collaboration
Borsanyi et al. 2010b
. Plots are adopted from
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
.

Figure 21:
Isentropes for different ratios of
s/n_{B}
in the phase diagram in the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model obtained in
Fu et al. 2020b
. Two cases with
\mu_{S}=0
and the strangeness neutrality
n_{S}=0
are compared. The black and gray lines stand for the chiral and deconfinement crossover lines, respectively. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020b
.

From the effective action in
Equation

125
or
Equation

146
, one is able to obtain the thermodynamic potential density, as follows

\displaystyle\Omega[T,\mu]=\frac{T}{V}\left(\Gamma_{k=0}[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}]\Big|_{T,\mu}-\Gamma_{k=0}[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}]\Big|_{T=\mu=0}\right)\,,

(161)

where
\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}
denotes the fields on the equations of motion. Then the pressure and the entropy density read

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=-\Omega[T,\mu]\,,

(162)

and

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\,.

(163)

Moreover, the trace anomaly that is also called as the interaction measure is given by

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\epsilon-3p\,,

(164)

where the energy density reads

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=-p+Ts+\sum_{f=u,d,s}\mu_{f}n_{f}\,,

(165)

with the number density for quark of flavor
f

\displaystyle n_{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial p}{\partial\mu_{f}}\,.

(166)

In
Figure

18
the reduced condensate, cf.
Equation

235
and the relevant discussions in
Section

IV.5
, is shown as a function of the reduced temperature
t=(T-T_{\mathrm{pc}})/T_{\mathrm{pc}}
obtained in
Herbst et al. 2014
, where
T_{\mathrm{pc}}
denotes the pseudocritical temperature for the chiral crossover. The fRG calculations are in agreement with the lattice simulations
Borsanyi et al. 2010a
. Furthermore, the mean-field results are also presented for comparison, where those labelled with “eMF” and “MF”
are obtained by taking into account or not the fermionic vacuum loop contribution in the calculations, respectively, and see, e.g.,
Schaefer and Wagner 2012
for more discussions. In
Figure

19
the pressure and the trace anomaly in the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QM model obtained in
Herbst et al. 2014
are shown as functions of the reduced temperature. The fRG results are compared with the lattice results
Bazavov 2013
;
Borsanyi et al. 2010b
, and it is observed that the fRG results are consistent with the lattice results from the Wuppertal-Budapest collaboration (WB), except that the trace anomaly from fRG is a bit larger than that from WB in the regime of high temperature with
t\gtrsim 0.3
. Moreover, the mean-field results as well as those including a contribution of the thermal pion gas, are also presented for comparison.

The equations of state in
Figure

19
are obtained in fRG within the local potential approximation (LPA), where the RG-scale dependence only enters the effective potential in
Equation

125
. The equations of state are also calculated beyond the LPA approximation in
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
. The relevant results are presented in
Figure

20
. In the calculations the wave function renormalizations for the mesons and quarks, and the RG scale dependence of the Yukawa coupling are taken into account. In
Figure

20
the results beyond LPA are in comparison to the lattice and LPA results. One can see that the trace anomaly is decreased a bit beyond LPA in the regime of
T\gtrsim 1.1T_{c}
. In
Figure

21
the influence of the strangeness neutrality on the isentropes is presented obtained in
Fu et al. 2020b
. It is found that, in comparison to the case of
\mu_{S}=0
, the isentropes with
n_{S}=0
move towards the r.h.s. a bit, and their turning around in the regime of crossover becomes more abruptly. Moreover, isentropes obtained from the Taylor expansion and the grid method for the effective potential are compared, and consistent results are found
Nakano et al. 2010
.

Note that calculations of the equations of state at high baryon densities within the fRG approach have also made progress recently, see, e.g.,
Leonhardt et al. 2020
;
Otto et al. 2020a
;
Otto et al. 2020b
, and the obtained EoS has been employed to study the phenomenology of compact stars
Otto et al. 2020a
;
Otto et al. 2020b
.

(left panels) and

Figure 29:
Correlation between the baryon number and the strangeness
C_{BS}
as a function of the temperature with several different values of the baryon chemical potential, obtained within the fRG approach in

. Two cases with
\mu_{S}=0
(dashed lines) and the strangeness neutrality
n_{S}=0
(solid lines) are compared. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2019
.

Differentiating the pressure in
Equation

162
with respect to the baryon chemical potential
n
times, one is led to the
n
-th order generalized susceptibility of the baryon number, i.e.,

\displaystyle\chi_{n}^{B}=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial(\mu_{B}/T)^{n}}\frac{p}{T^{4}}\,,

(167)

which is also related to the
n
-th order cumulant of the net baryon number distributions, that is,

\displaystyle\langle(\delta N_{B})^{n}\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{N_{B}=-\infty}^{\infty}(\delta N_{B})^{n}P(N_{B})\,.

(168)

with
\delta N_{B}=N_{B}-\langle N_{B}\rangle
. Here
P(N_{B})
denotes the probability distribution of the net baryon number, which can be calculated theoretically from the canonical ensemble with an imaginary chemical potential, and see, e.g.,
Morita et al. 2013
;
Sun et al. 2018
for a detailed discussion. In experimental measurements, since neutrons are difficult to detect, the net proton number distribution is measured, as a proxy for the net baryon number distribution, and see, e.g.,
Luo and Xu 2017
for more details. Evidently,
\langle N_{B}\rangle
is the ensemble average of the net baryon number.

The relations between the generalized susceptibilities in
Equation

167
and the cumulants in
Equation

168
for the lowest four orders read

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\braket{N_{B}}\,,

(169)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}\,,

(170)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{3}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}\,,

(171)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{4}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\Big(\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{4}}-3\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}^{2}\Big)\,,

(172)

which leaves us with the experimental observables as follows

\displaystyle M=

\displaystyle VT^{3}\chi_{1}^{\text{B}}\,,\qquad\sigma^{2}=VT^{3}\chi_{2}^{\text{B}}\,,

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\frac{\chi_{3}^{\text{B}}}{\chi_{2}^{\text{B}}\sigma}\,,\qquad\hskip 15.6491pt\kappa=\frac{\chi_{4}^{\text{B}}}{\chi_{2}^{\text{B}}\sigma^{2}}\,.

(173)

Here
M
,
\sigma^{2}
,
S
,
\kappa
stand for the mean value, the variance, the skewness, and the kurtosis of the net baryon or proton number distributions. In the same way, calculations can also be extended to the hyper-order baryon number fluctuations, i.e.,
\chi_{n>4}^{B}

Wagner et al. 2010
;
Karsch et al. 2011
;
Schaefer and Wagner 2012
;
Friman et al. 2011b
;
Fu et al. 2021
. The relations between the hyper-order susceptibilities and the cumulants are given by
Fu et al. 2021

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{5}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\Big(\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{5}}-10\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}\Big)\,,

(174)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{6}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\Big(\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{6}}-15\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{4}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}

\displaystyle-10\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}^{2}+30\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}^{3}\Big)\,,

(175)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{7}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\Big(\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{7}}-21\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{5}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}

\displaystyle-35\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{4}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}

\displaystyle+210\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}^{2}\Big)\,,

(176)

\displaystyle\chi^{B}_{8}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{VT^{3}}\Big(\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{8}}-28\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{6}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}

\displaystyle-56\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{5}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}-35\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{4}}^{2}

\displaystyle+420\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{4}}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+560\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{3}}^{2}\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}-630\braket{(\delta N_{B})^{2}}^{4}\Big)\,.

(177)

It is more convenient to adopt the ratio between two susceptibilities of different orders, say

\displaystyle R_{nm}^{B}=

\displaystyle\frac{\chi_{n}^{B}}{\chi_{m}^{B}}\,,

(178)

where the explicit volume dependence is removed. The baryon number fluctuations as well as fluctuations of other conserved charges, e.g., the electric charge and the strangeness, and correlations among these conserved charges, have been widely studied in literatures. For lattice simulations, see, e.g.,
Bazavov et al. 2012
;
Borsanyi et al. 2013
;
Borsanyi et al. 2014
;
Bazavov et al. 2017a
;
Bazavov et al. 2017b
;
Borsanyi et al. 2018
;
Bazavov et al. 2020
. Investigations of fluctuations and correlations within the fRG approach to LEFTs can be found in, e.g.,
Skokov et al. 2010
;
Skokov et al. 2011
;
Friman et al. 2011b
;
Morita et al. 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2016
;
Fu et al. 2016
;
Almasi et al. 2017b
;
Fu et al. 2020b
;
Fu et al. 2019
;
Wen et al. 2019
;
Wen and Fu 2021
;
Fu et al. 2021
, and within the mean-field approximations in, e.g.,
Fu et al. 2010
;
Fu and Wu 2010
;
Karsch et al. 2011
;
Schaefer and Wagner 2012
;
Li et al. 2019b
. For the relevant studies from Dyson-Schwinger Equations, see, e.g.,
Xin et al. 2014
;
Isserstedt et al. 2019
. Remarkably, recently QCD-assisted LEFTs with the fRG approach have been developed and used to study the skewness and kurtosis of the baryon number distributions
Fu and Pawlowski 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2016
;
Fu et al. 2016
, the baryon-strangeness correlations
Fu et al. 2020b
;
Fu et al. 2019
, and the hyper-order baryon number fluctuations
Fu et al. 2021
.

In
Figure

22
the kurtosis of the baryon number distributions is shown as a function of the temperature at vanishing chemical potential. In the calculations the frequency dependence of the quark wave function renormalization is taken into account
Fu et al. 2016
, whose contribution to the flow of the effective potential is shown schematically in
Figure

23
. It is found that a summation for the quark external frequency is indispensable to the Silver Blaze property at finite chemical potential, see
Fu et al. 2016
for a more detailed discussion. From
Figure

22
, one can see that the summation for the external frequency of the quark wave function renormalization improves the agreement between the fRG results and the lattice ones.

In
Figure

24
the fourth-, sixth-, and eighth-order baryon number fluctuations divided by the quadratic one are shown as functions of the temperature at vanishing baryon chemical potential. The fRG results
Fu et al. 2021
are compared with lattice results from the HotQCD collaboration
Bazavov et al. 2017a
;
Bazavov et al. 2017b
;
Bazavov et al. 2020
and the Wuppertal-Budapest collaboration (WB)
Borsanyi et al. 2018
. It is observed that the fRG results are in quantitative, qualitative agreement with the WB and HotQCD results, respectively. In
Figure

25
the baryon number fluctuations of different orders,
R^{B}_{31}
,
R^{B}_{42}
,
R^{B}_{51}
,
R^{B}_{62}
,
R^{B}_{71}
and
R^{B}_{82}
, are shown as functions of the temperature with several different values of the baryon chemical potential from 0 to 400 MeV. One finds that the dependence of fluctuations on the temperature oscillates more pronouncedly with the increasing order of cumulants. Moreover, with the increase of the baryon chemical potential the chiral crossover grows sharper, which leads to the increase of the magnitude of fluctuations significantly.

In the left panel of
Figure

26
the kurtosis of the baryon number distributions is depicted in the phase diagram
Fu et al. 2021
. The black dashed line denotes the chiral phase boundary in the crossover regime. The green dotted line stands for the chemical freeze-out curve obtained from the STAR freeze-out data
Adamczyk et al. 2017
, see
Fu et al. 2021
for a more detailed discussion. One can see that there is narrow blue area near the phase boundary with
\mu_{B}\gtrsim 300
MeV, which indicates that the kurtosis becomes negative in this area. Moreover, it is found that with the increase of the baryon chemical potential, the freeze-out curve moves towards the blue area firstly, and then deviates from it a bit at large baryon chemical potential. The skewness of the baryon number distributions as a function of the collision energy calculated in the fRG approach is presented in the right panel of
Figure

26

Fu et al. 2021
, which is in comparison to the STAR data of the skewness of the net-proton number distributions
R^{p}_{32}
with centrality 0-5%
Adam et al. 2020
. It is found that the fRG results are in good agreement with the experimental data except the two lowest energy points, which is attributed to the fact that other effects, e.g., volume fluctuations
Luo et al. 2013
;
Chatterjee et al. 2020a
;
Chatterjee et al. 2020b
, the global baryon number conservation
He et al. 2016
;
Braun-Munzinger et al. 2017
;
Vovchenko et al. 2020
, become important when the beam collision energy is low.

In
Figure

27
baryon number fluctuations
R^{B}_{42}
,
R^{B}_{62}
,
R^{B}_{82}
,
R^{B}_{31}
,
R^{B}_{51}
, and
R^{B}_{71}
are shown as functions of the collision energy obtained within the fRG approach
Fu et al. 2021
, where the chemical freeze-out curve obtained from STAR data as shown in the left panel of
Figure

26
is used. The fRG results are compared with experimental data of the kurtosis of the net-proton number distributions
R^{p}_{42}
with centrality 0-5%
Adam et al. 2021
, and the sixth-order cumulant
R^{p}_{62}
with centrality 0-10%
Abdallah et al. 2021a
. A non-monotonic dependence of the kurtosis
R^{B}_{42}
on the collision energy is also observed in the fRG calculations, which is consistent with the experimental measurements of
R^{p}_{42}
. Moreover, it is found that this non-monotonicity arises from the increasingly sharp crossover with the decrease of the collision energy, which is also reflected in the heat map of the kurtosis in the phase diagram in the left panel of
Figure

26
. The fRG results of
R^{B}_{62}
are also qualitatively consistent with the experimental data of
R^{p}_{62}
.

In
Figure

28
the full results of the baryon number fluctuations
R^{B}_{42}
and
R^{B}_{62}
are compared with those of Taylor expansion for the baryon chemical potential up to the eighth and tenth orders, which can be used to investigate the convergence properties of the Taylor expansion for the baryon chemical potential. For the two values of the temperature,
T=155
MeV and 160 MeV, it is found that the full results deviate from those of Taylor expansion significantly with
\mu_{B}/T\gtrsim 1.2\sim 1.5
. The oscillating behavior of the full results at large baryon chemical potential is not captured by the Taylor expansion, which hints that the convergence radius of the Taylor expansion for the baryon chemical potential might be restricted by some singularity, e.g., the Yang–Lee edge singularity, and see, e.g.,
Stephanov 2006b
;
Mukherjee and Skokov 2021
;
Connelly et al. 2020
;
Rennecke and Skokov 2022
for more discussions.

We close this subsection with a discussion about the correlation between the baryon number and the strangeness, i.e.,

\displaystyle C_{BS}=

\displaystyle-3\frac{\chi_{11}^{BS}}{\chi_{2}^{S}}\,,

(179)

which is calculated within the fRG approach in
Fu et al. 2019
. There, the influence of the strangeness neutrality on
C_{BS}
is investigated, and the relevant results are presented in
Figure

29
. One can see that the correlation between the baryon number and the strangeness is suppressed by the condition of the strangeness neutrality.
