Table 2:
Comparison of the critical exponents for the 3
d

O(4)
and
Z(2)
symmetry universality classes from different approaches. The values with an asterisk are derived from the scaling relations in
Equation

184
. The table is adopted from
Chen et al. 2021
.

Figure 30:
Critical exponent
\beta
for the 3
d

O(4)
(green lines) and
Z(2)
(red lines) symmetry universality classes extracted from different ranges of the temperature, obtained within the fRG approach in
Chen et al. 2021
. The conformal bootstrap results (gray dashed lines)
Kos et al. 2014
;
Kos et al. 2015
are also presented for comparison. The plot is adopted from
Chen et al. 2021
.

According the scaling argument, when a system is in the critical regime, its thermodynamic potential density can be decomposed into a sum of a singular and a regular part
Ma 2000
, viz.,

\displaystyle\Omega\big(t,h\big)

\displaystyle=f_{s}(t,h)+f_{\mathrm{reg}}(t,h)\,,

(180)

whereof, the singular part satisfies the scaling relation to the leading order as follows

\displaystyle f_{s}(t,h)

\displaystyle=\ell^{-d}f_{s}(t\,\ell^{y_{t}},\,h\,\ell^{y_{h}})\,,

(181)

with a dimensionless rescaling factor
\ell
and the spacial dimension
d
. In Eqs. (
180
) and (
181
)
t
and
h
stand for the reduced temperature and magnetic field, respectively. They are given by

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{T-T_{c}}{T_{0}}\,,\qquad h=\frac{c}{c_{0}}\,,

(182)

where
T_{c}
is the critical temperature in the chiral limit, and
T_{0}
and
c_{0}
are some normalization values for the temperature
T
and the strength of the explicit chiral symmetry breaking
c
as shown in
Equation

125
, respectively. In the following, the order parameter will be denoted as the magnetization density, and
c
as the magnetic field strength, i.e.,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\equiv\sigma\,,\qquad H\equiv c\,.

(183)

The scaling relation in
Equation

181
leaves us with a set of scaling relations for various critical exponents, cf.
Ma 2000
;
Braun and Klein 2008
, such as,

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\nu}\,,\quad\!\!y_{h}=\frac{\beta\delta}{\nu}\,,\quad\!\!\beta=\frac{\nu}{2}(d-2+\eta)\,,\quad\!\!\gamma=\beta(\delta-1)\,,

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=(2-\eta)\nu\,,\quad\delta=\frac{d+2-\eta}{d-2+\eta}\,,\quad\nu d=\beta(1+\delta)\,.

(184)

The critical behavior of the order parameter is described by the critical exponents
\beta
and
\delta
, which read

\displaystyle M(t,h=0)

\displaystyle\sim(-t)^{\beta}\,,

(185)

with
t<0
, and

\displaystyle M(t=0,h)

\displaystyle\sim h^{1/\delta}\,.

(186)

Moreover, one has the susceptibility of the order parameter
\chi
and the correlation length
\xi
, which scale as

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\sim|t|^{-\gamma}\,,\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\xi\sim|t|^{-\nu}\,.

(187)

Recently, the pseudo-spectral method of the Chebyshev expansion for the effective potential has been used to calculate the critical exponents
Chen et al. 2021
. In the
N_{f}=2
QM model within the fRG approach, the critical exponents for the 3
d

O(4)
and
Z(2)
symmetry universality classes, which correspond to the black dashed
O(4)
phase transition line and the red dashed
Z(2)
phase transition line as shown in
Figure

14
respectively, are calculated with the Chebyshev expansion of the effective potential. Both the LPA and LPA
′
approximations are used in the calculations. In the LPA
′
approximation a field-dependent mesonic wave function renormalization is taken into account. The relevant results are show in
Table

2
, which are also compared with results of critical exponents from other approaches, e.g., Taylor expansion of the effective potential
Tetradis and Wetterich 1994
;
Von Gersdorff and Wetterich 2001
;
Litim and Pawlowski 2001
;
Braun and Klein 2008
;
Stokic et al. 2010
and the grid method
Bohr et al. 2001
within the fRG approach, the derivative expansions (DE) up to orders of
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{4})
and
\mathcal{O}(\partial^{6})
with the fRG approach
Balog et al. 2019
;
De Polsi et al. 2020
, the conformal bootstrap for the 3
d
conformal field theories (CFTs)
Kos et al. 2014
;
Kos et al. 2015
, Monte Carlo simulations
Kanaya and Kaya 1995
, and the
d=3
perturbation expansion
Zinn-Justin 2001
. In the mean time, the mean-field values of the critical exponents are also shown in the last line of
Table

2
.

In
Figure

30
the critical exponent
\beta
for the 3
d

O(4)
and
Z(2)
symmetry universality classes extracted from different ranges of the temperature is shown, which is obtained with the Chebyshev expansion for the effective potential in the fRG
Chen et al. 2021
. It is found that only when the temperature is very close to the critical temperature, say
|T-T_{c}|\lesssim 0.01
MeV, the fRG results of both the
O(4)
and
Z(2)
symmetry universality classes are consistent with the conformal bootstrap results. This indicates that the size of the critical region is extremely small, far smaller than
\sim 1
MeV. Similar estimates for the size of the critical region are also found in, e.g.,
Schaefer and Wambach 2007
;
Braun and Klein 2008
;
Braun et al. 2011a
;
Klein 2017
.

IV
QCD at finite temperature and density

In this section we would like to present a brief review on recent progresses in studies of QCD at finite temperature and densities within the fRG approach to the first-principle QCD, which are mainly based on the work in
Fu et al. 2020a
;
Braun et al. 2020a
. The relevant fRG flows at finite temperature and densities there have been developed from the counterparts in the vacuum in
Braun et al. 2016a
;
Rennecke 2015a
. Furthermore, these flows are also built upon the state-of-the-art quantitative fRG results for Yang-Mills theory in the vacuum
Cyrol et al. 2016
and at finite temperature
Cyrol et al. 2018a
, QCD in the vacuum
Mitter et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
.

In the following we focus on the chiral phase transition. As mentioned above, the QCD phase transitions involve both the chiral phase transition and the confinement-deconfinement phase transition. In fact, related subjects of the deconfinement phase transition, such as the Wilson loop, the Polyakov loop, the flows of the effective action of a background gauge field, the relation between the confinement and correlation functions, etc., have been widely studied within the fRG approach to Yang-Mills theory and QCD. Significant progresses have been made in relevant studies, see
Dupuis et al. 2021
for a recent overview, and also e.g.
Schaefer et al. 2002
;
Pawlowski 2003
;
Fischer and Gies 2004
;
Fischer and Pawlowski 2007
;
Braun et al. 2010a
;
Fischer et al. 2009
;
Fischer and Pawlowski 2009
;
Braun et al. 2011b
;
Braun et al. 2010b
;
Eichhorn et al. 2011
;
Fister and Pawlowski 2011
;
Fister and Pawlowski 2013
;
Fischer et al. 2014a
;
Haas et al. 2013
;
Herbst et al. 2015
;
Corell et al. 2018
;
Horak et al. 2022a
;
Pawlowski et al. 2022
.

IV.1
Propagators and anomalous dimensions

Figure 31:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equations for the inverse quark, gluon and meson propagators, respectively, where the dashed lines stand for the mesons and the dotted lines denote the ghost.

Figure 32:
Quark (left panel,
Z_{q}
) and mesonic (right panel,
\bar{Z}_{\phi}
) wave function renormalizations of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD obtained in the fRG with RG scale
k=0
, as functions of the temperature with different values of the baryon chemical potential. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 33:
Gluon dressing functions
1/Z_{A}(p)
of
N_{f}=2
and
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the vacuum as functions of the momentum, where the fRG results are in comparison to lattice calculations of
N_{f}=2
flavors
Sternbeck et al. 2012
and
N_{f}=2+1
flavors
Zafeiropoulos et al. 2019
;
Boucaud et al. 2018
.The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 34:
Gluon dressing function
1/Z_{A}(p)
and the gluon propagator
G_{A}=1/[Z_{A}(p)\,p^{2}]
(inlay) as functions of momenta for QCD of
N_{f}=2+1
flavors in the vacuum, where the fRG results denoted by the black lines are compared with the continuum extrapolated lattice results by the RBC/UKQCD collaboration, see e.g.,
Blum et al. 2016
;
Zafeiropoulos et al. 2019
;
Boucaud et al. 2018
. For the fRG results the momentum dependence is given by
p^{2}=k^{2}
. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 35:
Gluon dressing function
1/Z_{A}
of the
N_{f}=2+1
QCD as a function of spatial momenta
|{\bm{p}}|
with several different values of temperature (left panel) and baryon chemical potential (right panel), where the Matsubara frequency
p_{0}
is chosen to be vanishing. The identification
\bm{p}^{2}=k^{2}
is used. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 36:
Gluon dressing function
1/Z_{A}
of
N_{f}=2+1
flavors as a function of spatial momenta
|{\bm{p}}|
at several values of temperature obtained in fRG, which is also compared with the lattice results of
N_{f}=2+1+1
in
Ilgenfritz et al. 2018
. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

The flow equations of inverse propagators are obtained by taking the second derivative of the Wetterich equation in
Equation

83
with respect to respective fields. The resulting flow equations of the inverse quark, gluon and meson propagators are shown diagrammatically in
Figure

31
. Making appropriate projections for these flow equations, one is able to arrive at the flow of the wave function renormalization
Z_{\Phi,k}
as shown in
Equation

51
for a given field
\Phi
, which includes nontrivial dispersion relation for the field, and is more conveniently reformulated as the anomalous dimension as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{\Phi,k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial_{t}Z_{\Phi,k}}{Z_{\Phi,k}}\,.

(188)

The quark two-point correlation function defined in
Equation

87
, see also
Equation

345
, reads

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k}(p)=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\bar{q}(-p)\delta q(p)}\bigg|_{\Phi=\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{q,k}(p)i\gamma\cdot p+m_{q,k}(p)\,,

(189)

where
\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}
denotes the fields in
Equation

65
on their respective equations of motion, that are vanishing except the
\sigma
field and the temporal gluon field
A_{0}
. Note that the minus sign on the r.h.s. of
Equation

189
, while not appearing in
Equation

345
, is due to the fact that the right derivative is used in
Equation

345
. Moreover, the quark chemical potentials in
Equation

50
have been assumed to be vanishing in
Equation

189
. Apparently, the quark wave function renormalization and mass are readily obtained by projecting
Equation

189
onto the vector and scalar channels, respectively, which read

\displaystyle Z_{q,k}(p)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4i}\frac{1}{p^{2}}\operatorname{tr}\Big(\gamma\cdot p\,\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k}(p)\Big)

\displaystyle m_{q,k}(p)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}\Big(\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k}(p)\Big)\,,

(190)

where the trace runs only in the Dirac space. Neglecting the dependence of the quark wave function renormalization on the spacial momentum
\bm{p}
, one arrives at the quark anomalous dimension as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{q,k}(p_{0})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4Z_{q,k}(p_{0})}

\displaystyle\times\mathrm{Re}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial(|\bm{p}|^{2})}\mathrm{tr}\left(i\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{p}\big(\partial_{t}\Gamma^{(2)\bar{q}q}_{k}(p)\big)\right)\right]_{\bm{p}=0}\,,

(191)

where the computation is done at
\bm{p}=0
, and
p_{0}
is a small, but nonvanishing frequency. The nontrivial choice of
p_{0}
and the fact that the expression in the square bracket in
Equation

191
is complex-valued at nonzero chemical potentials, are both related to constraints from the Silver-Blaze property for the correlation functions at finite chemical potentials, and see, e.g.,
Markó et al. 2014
;
Khan et al. 2015
;
Fu and Pawlowski 2016
;
Fu et al. 2016
;
Fu et al. 2020a
for more details. In
Equation

191
Re denotes that the real part of the expression in the square bracket is adopted. Note that in
Equation

191
the difference between the parts of the quark wave function renormalization longitudinal and transversal to the heat bath is ignored, where the projection is done on the spacial component, and thus the transversal anomalous dimension is used. The explicit expression of
\eta_{q,k}
is given in
Equation

426
. In the left panel of
Figure

32
, the quark wave function renormalization
Z_{q,k=0}(p_{0},\bm{p}=0)
is depicted as a function of the temperature with different values of the baryon chemical potential.

The mesonic two-point correlation functions, i.e., those of the
\pi
and
\sigma
fields, are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)\pi\pi}_{k}(p)=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\pi_{i}(-p)\delta\pi_{j}(p)}\bigg|_{\Phi=\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(Z_{\pi,k}(p)p^{2}+m_{\pi,k}^{2}\big)\delta_{ij}\,,

(192)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2)\sigma\sigma}_{k}(p)=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\sigma(-p)\delta\sigma(p)}\bigg|_{\Phi=\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{\sigma,k}(p)p^{2}+m_{\sigma,k}^{2}\,,

(193)

respectively, where the curvature masses of the mesons read

\displaystyle m_{\pi,k}^{2}=

\displaystyle V_{k}^{\prime}(\rho)\,,\qquad m_{\sigma,k}^{2}=

\displaystyle V_{k}^{\prime}(\rho)+2\rho V_{k}^{(2)}(\rho)\,.

(194)

As same as the quark wave function renormalization, the thermal splitting of the mesonic wave function renormalization in parts longitudinal and transversal to the heat bath is neglected. Moreover, one has assumed a unique renormalization for both the pion and sigma fields, viz.,
Z_{\phi,k}=Z_{\pi,k}=Z_{\sigma,k}
. The validity of these approximations have been verified seriously in
Yin et al. 2019
. The anomalous dimension of mesons at vanishing frequency
p_{0}=0
and a finite spacial momentum
\bm{p}
is given by

\displaystyle\eta_{\phi}(0,\bm{p})=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{ij}}{3Z_{\phi}(0,\bm{p})}\frac{\partial_{t}\Gamma_{\pi_{i}\pi_{j}}^{(2)}(0,\bm{p})-\partial_{t}\Gamma_{\pi_{i}\pi_{j}}^{(2)}(0,0)}{\bm{p}^{2}}\,,

(195)

Two cases are of special interest: One is the choice
\bm{p}^{2}=k^{2}
, i.e.,
\eta_{\phi}(0,k)
, which captures the most momentum dependence of the mesonic two-point correlation functions, and thus is usually used in the r.h.s. of flow equations involving mesonic degrees of freedom, for more detailed discussions see
Fu et al. 2020a
. The anomalous dimension
\eta_{\phi}(0,k)
leaves us with the renormalization constant defined as follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\bar{Z}_{\phi,k}}\partial_{t}\bar{Z}_{\phi,k}\equiv-\eta_{\phi}(0,k)\,,\quad\textrm{with}\quad\bar{Z}_{\phi,k=\Lambda}=1\,.

(196)

In the right panel of
Figure

32
,
\bar{Z}_{\phi,k=0}
is depicted as a function of the temperature with different values of the baryon chemical potentials. The other is the case
\bm{p}=0
, and
Equation

195
is reduced the equation as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{\phi}(0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3Z_{\phi}(0)}\delta_{ij}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{p}^{2}}\partial_{t}\Gamma^{(2)}_{\pi_{i}\pi_{j}}\right]_{p=0}\,.

(197)

The related wave function renormalization
Z_{\phi,k}(0)
is used to extract the renormalized meson mass as

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{\pi,k=0}}{\sqrt{Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)}}\,,\qquad\bar{m}_{\sigma}=\frac{m_{\sigma,k=0}}{\sqrt{Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)}}\,,

(198)

where
\bar{m}_{\pi}
and
\bar{m}_{\sigma}
are approximately equal to their pole masses, respectively. The explicit expressions for
Equation

197
and
Equation

195
are presented in
Equation

427
and
Equation

428
, respectively.

For the ghost anomalous dimension, one extracts it from the relation as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{c}=

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{p\partial_{p}Z^{\mathrm{QCD}}_{c,k=0}(p)}{Z^{\mathrm{QCD}}_{c,k=0}(p)}\right|_{p=k}\,,

(199)

where
Z^{\mathrm{QCD}}_{c,k=0}(p)
denotes the momentum-dependent ghost wave function renormalization in QCD with
N_{f}=2
flavors in the vacuum obtained in
Cyrol et al. 2018b
. Note that the ghost propagator is found to be very insensitive to the effects of finite temperature as well as the quark contributions for
N_{f}=2
and
N_{f}=2+1
flavors, see e.g.
Cyrol et al. 2018a
.

The gluon anomalous dimension is decomposed into a sum of three parts, as follows

\displaystyle\eta_{A}=

\displaystyle\eta_{A,\mathrm{vac}}^{\mathrm{QCD}}+\Delta\eta_{A}^{\mathrm{glue}}+\Delta\eta_{A}^{q}\,,

(200)

where the first term on the r.h.s. denotes the gluon anomalous dimension in the vacuum, and the other two terms stand for the medium contributions to the gluon anomalous dimension from the glue (gluons and ghosts) and quark loops, respectively. The vacuum contribution in
Equation

200
is further expressed as

\displaystyle\eta_{A,\mathrm{vac}}^{\mathrm{QCD}}=

\displaystyle\left.\eta_{A,\mathrm{vac}}^{\mathrm{QCD}}\right|_{N_{f}=2}+\eta^{s}_{A,\mathrm{vac}}\,,

(201)

where the two terms on the r.h.s. denote the contributions from the light quarks of
N_{f}=2
flavors and the strange quark. The former one is inferred from the gluon dressing function
Z^{\textrm{QCD}}_{A,k=0}(p)
for the
N_{f}=2
flavor QCD in
Cyrol et al. 2018b
, which reads

\displaystyle\left.\eta_{A,\mathrm{vac}}^{\mathrm{QCD}}\right|_{N_{f}=2}=

\displaystyle-\left.\frac{p\partial_{p}Z^{\mathrm{QCD}}_{A,k=0}(p)}{Z^{\mathrm{QCD}}_{A,k=0}(p)}\right|_{p=k}\,.

(202)

The explicit expressions of
\Delta\eta_{A}^{q}
in
Equation

200
and
\eta^{s}_{A,\mathrm{vac}}
in
Equation

201
can be found in
Equation

429
,
Equation

430
,
Equation

431
. Moreover, the in-medium contribution to the gluon anomalous dimension resulting from the glue sector, i.e., the second term on the r.h.s. of
Equation

200
, is taken into account in
Equation

432
.

In
Figure

33
the gluon dressing functions
1/Z_{A}(p)
of
N_{f}=2
and
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the vacuum are shown. The fRG and lattice results are presented. Here the fRG gluon dressing of
N_{f}=2
flavors is inputted from
Cyrol et al. 2018b
, as shown in
Equation

202
, which is also in quantitative agreement with the lattice result in
Sternbeck et al. 2012
. Note that the gluon dressing of
N_{f}=2+1
flavors here is a genuine prediction, which is in good agreement with the respective lattice results
Zafeiropoulos et al. 2019
;
Boucaud et al. 2018
. In
Figure

34
both the gluon dressing function and the gluon propagator of
N_{f}=2+1
flavors are presented. The calculated gluon dressing functions at finite temperature and baryon chemical potential in fRG are shown in
Figure

35
. In the left panel of
Figure

35
several different values of temperature are chosen with
\mu_{B}=0
, and it is found that the gluon dressing function
1/Z_{A}
decreases with the increase of temperature. In the right panel, several different values of
\mu_{B}
are adopted while with
T=150
MeV fixed. It is observed that the dependence of the gluon dressing function on the baryon chemical potential is very small. The gluon dressing functions at finite temperature obtained in fRG are compared with the relevant lattice results from
Ilgenfritz et al. 2018
in
Figure

36
, where several different values of temperature are chosen. One can see that the gluon dressing at finite temperature in fRG is comparable to that of lattice QCD.

IV.2
Strong couplings

Figure 37:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equations for the quark-gluon, three-gluon, and the quark-meson vertices, respectively.

Figure 38:
Quark-gluon couplings for light quarks (
\alpha_{\bar{l}lA}
) and strange quarks (
\alpha_{\bar{s}sA}
), and the three-gluon coupling (
\alpha_{A^{3}}
) of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD as functions of the RG scale
k
at several values of the temperature and vanishing baryon chemical potential. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

The quark-gluon coupling in
Equation

55
, the ghost-gluon coupling in
Equation

56
, and the three-gluon or four-gluon coupling in
Equation

52
, etc., are consistent with one another in the perturbative regime of high energy, due to the Slavnov-Taylor identities (STIs) resulting from the gauge symmetry. However, when the momenta or the RG scale are decreased to
k\lesssim 1\sim 3\,
GeV, the gluon mass gap begins to affect the running of strong couplings, and also the transversal strong couplings can not be identified with the longitudinal ones via the modified STIs, see, e.g.,
Cornwall 1982
;
Aguilar et al. 2011
;
Cyrol et al. 2016
;
Pawlowski et al. 2022
for more details. Consequently, different strong couplings deviate from one another in the low energy regime of
k\lesssim 1\sim 3\,
GeV
Mitter et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2016
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
;
Cyrol et al. 2018a
, and thus it is necessary to distinguish them by adding appropriate suffixes. The couplings of the purely gluonic sector read

\displaystyle\alpha_{A^{3},k}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\lambda_{A^{3},k}^{2}}{Z_{A,k}^{3}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{A^{4},k}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\lambda_{A^{4},k}}{Z_{A,k}^{2}}\,,

(203)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\bar{c}cA,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\lambda_{\bar{c}cA,k}^{2}}{Z_{A,k}\,Z_{c,k}^{2}}\,,

(204)

where
\lambda_{A^{3},k}
,
\lambda_{A^{4},k}
and
\lambda_{\bar{c}cA,k}
denote the three-gluon, four-gluon, ghost-gluon dressing functions, respectively, as shown in
Equation

363
,
Equation

369
,
Equation

357
. The couplings of the matter sector read

\displaystyle\alpha_{\bar{l}lA,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\lambda_{\bar{l}lA,k}^{2}}{Z_{A,k}\,Z_{q,k}^{2}}\,,\qquad\alpha_{\bar{s}sA,k}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{\lambda_{\bar{s}sA,k}^{2}}{Z_{A,k}\,Z_{q,k}^{2}}\,,

(205)

where the light-quark-gluon coupling and the strange-quark-gluon coupling have been distinguished. From the calculated results in, e.g.,
Braun et al. 2016a
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
, it is reasonable to adopt the approximation as follows

\displaystyle\alpha_{A^{4},k}=\alpha_{A^{3},k}\,,\qquad\alpha_{\bar{c}cA,k}\simeq\alpha_{\bar{l}lA,k}\,.

(206)

The flow equation of the quark-gluon vertex is presented in the first line of
Figure

37
. Projecting onto the classical tensor structure of the quark-gluon vertex, denoted by

\displaystyle\big(S^{(3)}_{\bar{q}qA}\big)^{a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\equiv-i\gamma_{\mu}t^{a}\,,

(207)

one is led to the flow of the quark-gluon coupling as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\bar{q}qA,k}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\eta_{A}+\eta_{q}\right)\bar{g}_{\bar{q}qA,k}+\frac{1}{8(N_{c}^{2}-1)}

\displaystyle\times\operatorname{tr}\,\left[\left(\overline{{\mathrm{Flow}}}^{(3)}_{\bar{q}qA}\right)^{a}_{\mu}\left(S^{{(3)}}_{\bar{q}qA}\right)^{a}_{\mu}\right]\big(\{p\})\,,

(208)

where the trace sums over the Dirac and color spaces, and
\{p\}
stands for the set of external momenta for the vertex. Here
g_{\bar{q}qA,k}\equiv\lambda_{\bar{q}qA,k}
is used. In
Equation

208
the flow of quark-gluon vertex, i.e., the r.h.s. of the first line in
Figure

37
, is denoted by

\displaystyle\left(\overline{{\mathrm{Flow}}}^{(3)}_{\bar{q}qA}\right)^{a}_{\mu}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{Z_{A,k}^{1/2}Z_{q,k}}\left(\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k}^{(3)\bar{q}qA}\right)^{a}_{\mu}\,,

(209)

with

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma_{k}^{(3)\bar{q}qA}\right)^{a}_{\mu}\equiv\frac{\delta}{\delta A^{c}_{\mu}}\frac{\overrightarrow{\delta}}{\delta\bar{q}}\Gamma_{k}\frac{\overleftarrow{\delta}}{\delta q}\,.

(210)

Note that besides the classical tensor structure of the quark-gluon vertex in
Equation

207
, some nonclassical tensor structures also play a sizable role in the dynamical breaking of the chiral symmetry
Mitter et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2018b
;
Vujinovic and Alkofer 2018
, see, e.g.,
Fu et al. 2020a
for more detailed discussions. From
Equation

208
one formulates the flow of the light-quark–gluon coupling as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\bar{l}lA,k}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\eta_{A}+\eta_{q}\right)\bar{g}_{\bar{l}lA,k}+\overline{\textrm{Flow}}^{(3),A}_{(\bar{l}lA)}+\overline{\textrm{Flow}}^{(3),\phi}_{(\bar{l}lA)}\,,

(211)

where the second term on the r.h.s. corresponds to the two diagrams on the r.h.s. of the flow equation for the quark-gluon vertex as shown in the first line of
Figure

37
, and the last term to the last diagram, which arise from the quark-gluon, quark-meson fluctuations, respectively. For the strange-quark–gluon coupling, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{\bar{s}sA,k}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\eta_{A}+\eta_{q}\right)\bar{g}_{\bar{s}sA,k}+\overline{\textrm{Flow}}^{(3),A}_{(\bar{s}sA)}\,,

(212)

where the contribution from the strange-quark–meson interactions are neglected, that is reasonable due to relatively larger masses of the strange quark and strange mesons. The explicit expressions in Eqs. (
211
) and (
212
) can be found in
Equation

434
and
Equation

435
.

In the same way, the flow equation of the three-gluon coupling,
g_{A^{3},k}\equiv\lambda_{A^{3},k}
, is readily obtained from the flow of the three-gluon vertex in the second line of
Figure

37
. The flow of the three-gluon coupling is decomposed into a sum of the vacuum part and the in-medium contribution, as follows

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{A^{3},k}=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{g}_{A^{3},k}^{\mathrm{vac}}+\partial_{t}\Delta\bar{g}_{A^{3},k}\,,

(213)

where the vacuum part, i.e., the first term on the r.h.s. is computed in
Braun et al. 2016a
, and the second term is identified with the in-medium contribution of the quark-gluon coupling, to wit,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta\bar{g}_{A^{3},k}=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta\bar{g}_{\bar{l}lA,k}\,.

(214)

In
Figure

38
the light-quark–gluon coupling, the strange-quark–gluon coupling, and the three-gluon coupling are depicted as functions of the RG scale for several different values of the temperature. It is observed that different couplings are consistent with one another in the regime of
k\gtrsim 3
GeV, while deviations develop in the nonperturbative or even semiperturbative scale. Moreover, one can see that the strong couplings decrease with the increasing temperature.

IV.3
Dynamical hadronization, four-quark couplings and Yukawa couplings

Figure 39:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equation for the four-quark vertex. The first line on the r.h.s. denotes the contributions from two gluon exchanges and the second line those from two meson exchanges. The last line stands for the contributions from the mixed diagrams with one gluon exchange and one meson exchange.

Figure 40:
Left panel: Comparison between the flow of the four-quark coupling in the
\sigma-\pi
channel from two gluon exchanges,
\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),A}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}
in
Equation

220
, as shown in the first line in
Figure

39
, and that from two meson exchanges,
\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),\phi}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}
, in the second line. The flows are depicted as functions of the RG scale with
T=0
and
\mu_{B}=0
. Right panel: Absolute values of the ratios
\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),A}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}/\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}
and
\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),\phi}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}/\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}
as functions of the RG scale. The position where
\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),\phi}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}
changes sign is labeled by the blue vertical line in both plots. The plots are adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 41:
Left panel: Renormalized Yukawa coupling of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD at vanishing RG scale
k=0
as a function of the temperature with several different values of
\mu_{B}
obtained in fRG. Right panel: Renormalized Yukawa coupling of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD as a function of the RG scale with several different values of
T
and
\mu_{B}=0
. The plots are adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 42:
Effective four-quark coupling of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the pseudoscalar channel
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(2\bar{m}_{\pi,k}^{2})
as a function of the RG scale with several different values of temperature and
\mu_{B}=0
. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 43:
Effective four-quark coupling of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the pseudoscalar channel at vanishing RG scale
\bar{h}_{k=0}^{2}/(2\bar{m}_{\pi,k=0}^{2})
as a function of the temperature with several different values of baryon chemical potential. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Following
Section

II.5
, one performs the dynamical hadronization for the
\sigma-\pi
channel, and use

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}\hat{\phi}_{k}\rangle=

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{k}\,\bar{q}\tau q\,,

(215)

with
\tau=(T^{0},i\gamma_{5}\bm{T})
as shown in
Equation

50
, where
\dot{A}_{k}
is called as the hadronization function. It is more convenient to adopt the dimensionless, renormalized hadronization function and four-quark coupling

\displaystyle\dot{\tilde{A}}=

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{\phi,k}^{1/2}}{Z_{q,k}}k^{2}\dot{A}_{k}\,,\qquad\tilde{\lambda}_{q,k}=\frac{k^{2}\lambda_{q,k}}{Z_{q,k}^{2}}\,,

(216)

and the renormalized Yukawa coupling,

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{k}=

\displaystyle\frac{h_{k}}{Z_{\phi,k}^{1/2}Z_{q,k}}\,.

(217)

Inserting the effective action in
Equation

50
into
Equation

83
and performing a projection on the four-quark interaction in the
\sigma-\pi
channel, one is left with

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{\lambda}_{q,k}=

\displaystyle 2(1+\eta_{q,k})\tilde{\lambda}_{q,k}+\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}+\dot{\tilde{A}}\,\bar{h}_{k}\,,

(218)

where

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}\equiv

\displaystyle-\frac{k^{2}}{Z_{q,k}^{2}}\left(\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k,(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}^{(4)}\right)_{\dot{A}_{k}=0}\,,

(219)

is the flow of the four-quark coupling in the
\sigma-\pi
channel, whose contributions have been depicted in
Figure

39
. One can see that the contributed diagrams can be classified into three sets, which correspond to the three lines on the r.h.s. of the flow equation in
Figure

39
. The first line is comprised of diagrams with two gluon exchanges, and the second line with two meson exchanges. The last line denotes the contributions from the mixed diagrams with one gluon exchange and one meson exchange. The mixed diagrams are negligible, since the dynamics of gluons and mesons dominate in different regimes of the RG scale, as shown in
Figure

40
. Therefore, the four-quark flow in
Equation

219
can be further written as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}=

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),A}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}+\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4),\phi}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}\,,

(220)

where the two terms on the r.h.s. correspond to the contributions from the two gluon exchanges and two meson exchanges, respectivley. Their explicit expressions are presented in
Equation

436
and
Equation

437
. In
Figure

40
the two terms on the r.h.s. of
Equation

220
and their ratios with respect to the total flow of the four-quark coupling are depicted as functions of the RG scale with
T=0
and
\mu_{B}=0
. It is observed that the flow resulting from the gluon exchange is dominant in the region of high energy, while that from the meson exchange plays a significant role in lower energy.

The dynamical hadronization is done by demanding

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{q,k}=

\displaystyle 0\,,

(221)

for every value of the RG scale
k
, which is equivalent to the fact that the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation is performed for every value of
k
. Thus from
Equation

218
one arrives at the hadronization function as follows

\displaystyle\dot{\tilde{A}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\bar{h}_{k}}\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(4)}_{(\bar{q}\tau q)^{2}}\,.

(222)

Inserting the effective action in
Equation

50
into
Equation

83
and performing a projection on the Yukawa interactions between quarks and
\sigma
-
\pi
mesons, i.e.,
\bar{q}\tau\cdot\phi q
, one is led to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{h}_{k}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\phi,k}+\eta_{q,k}\right)\bar{h}_{k}+\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(3)}_{(\bar{q}\bm{\tau}q)\bm{\pi}}-\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k}\dot{\tilde{A}}\,,

(223)

with the dimensionless and renormalized pion mass as follows

\displaystyle\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}_{\pi,k}}{Z_{\phi,k}k^{2}}\,.

(224)

Here in
Equation

223

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Flow}}^{(3)}_{(\bar{q}\bm{\tau}q)\bm{\pi}}\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\phi,k}^{1/2}Z_{q,k}}\left(\partial_{t}\Gamma_{k,(\bar{q}\bm{\tau}q)\bm{\pi}}^{(3)}\right)_{\dot{A}_{k}=0}\,,

(225)

is the flow of the Yukawa coupling between the pion and quarks, which is shown in the third line of
Figure

37
. Its explicit expression is presented in
Equation

438
. Therefore, substituting the hadronization function in
Equation

222
into
Equation

223
, one obtains the total flow of the Yukawa coupling finally. Evidently, the dynamics of resonance of quarks are stored in the interactions between quarks and mesons through the dynamic hadronization.

In the left panel of
Figure

41
the renormalized Yukawa coupling with
k=0
is shown as a function of the temperature with several different values of baryon chemical potential. It is observed that with the increase of
T
or
\mu_{B}
, the Yukawa coupling decays rapidly due to the restoration of the chiral symmetry. In the right panel of
Figure

41
the dependence of the renormalized Yukawa coupling on the RG scale for different values of temperature is investigated. One can see that the Yukawa coupling is stable in the region of
k\gtrsim 1
GeV, and the effects of temperature play a role approximately in
k\lesssim 2\pi T
. The effective four-quark coupling in the
\sigma
-
\pi
channel can be described by the ratio
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(2\bar{m}_{\pi,k}^{2})

Braun et al. 2016a
;
Fu et al. 2020a
, which is shown as a function of the RG scale with several different values of temperature and
\mu_{B}=0
in
Figure

42
. It is observed that with the decrease of
k
and entering the regime of the chiral symmetry breaking, a resonance occurs in the scalar-pseudoscalar channel, which results in a rapid increase of the effective four-quark coupling. Moreover, the dependence of the effective four-quark coupling on the baryon chemical potential is shown in
Figure

43
. One finds that the effective four-quark coupling decrease with the increasing temperature or baryon chemical potential.

IV.4
Natural emergence of LEFTs from QCD

Figure 44:
Four-quark single gluon exchange coupling for light quarks
\bar{g}^{2}_{\bar{l}lA,k}
and strange quarks
\bar{g}^{2}_{\bar{s}sA,k}
, dimensionless four-quark single meson exchange coupling
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(1+\tilde{m}_{\pi,k}^{2})
and
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(1+\tilde{m}_{\sigma,k}^{2})
as functions of the RG scale, obtained in fRG for the
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the vacuum. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 45:
Dimensionless propagator gappings
1/(1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\Phi_{i},k})
for
\Phi_{i}=l,s,\sigma,\pi
as functions of the RG scale. The gluon dressing function
1/Z_{A,k}
is also shown for comparison, which is normalized by its peak value
1/Z_{A}^{\mathrm{peak}}
at
k=k_{\mathrm{peak}}
. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

The fRG approach to QCD with the dynamical hadronization discussed above, provide us with a method to study the transition of degrees of freedom from fundamental to composite ones. One is also able to observe a natural emergence of LEFTs from original QCD. To that end, in
Figure

44
one shows the four-quark single gluon exchange coupling for light quarks
\bar{g}^{2}_{\bar{l}lA,k}
and strange quarks
\bar{g}^{2}_{\bar{s}sA,k}
, dimensionless four-quark single meson exchange coupling
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(1+\tilde{m}_{\pi,k}^{2})
and
\bar{h}_{k}^{2}/(1+\tilde{m}_{\sigma,k}^{2})
as functions of the RG scale in the vacuum. Evidently, it is found that the gluonic exchange couplings are dominant in the perturbative regime of
k\gtrsim 1
GeV. However, with the decrease of the RG scale the active dynamic is taken over gradually by the mesonic degrees of freedom, and one can see that the gluonic couplings and the Yukawa couplings are comparable to each other at
k\approx 600
. In
Figure

45
dimensionless propagator gappings
1/(1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\Phi_{i},k})
for
\Phi_{i}=l,s,\sigma,\pi
are shown as functions of the RG scale. The gluon dressing function is also show there for comparison. In
Figure

45
one observes the same information on decouplings as in
Figure

44
, that is, as the RG scale evolves from the UV towards IR, the gluons decouple from the matter at first, then the quarks, and the mesons finally. For more related discussions see
Fu et al. 2020a
.

IV.5
Chiral condensate

Figure 46:
Left panel: Renormalized light quark chiral condensate
\Delta_{l,R}
of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD as a function of the temperature with several different values of baryon chemical potential obtained in fRG, in comparison to the lattice results at vanishing
\mu_{B}

Borsanyi et al. 2010a
. Right panel: Derivative of
\Delta_{l,R}
with respect to the temperature, i.e., the thermal susceptibility of the renormalized light quark condensate, as a function of the temperature with several different values of baryon chemical potential. The plots are adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 47:
Reduced condensate of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD as a function of the temperature with
\mu_{B}=0
obtained in fRG, where the constituent quark mass differences are
\Delta\bar{m}_{sl}=\bar{m}_{s}-\bar{m}_{l}=120,150,155,160
MeV, corresponding to the current quark mass ratios of
m_{s}^{0}/m_{l}^{0}=c_{\sigma_{s}}/c_{\sigma}\approx 14,27,30,34
. The fRG results are compared with the lattice ones in
Borsanyi et al. 2010a
. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

The chiral condensate of quark
q_{i}=u,d,s
reads

\displaystyle\Delta_{q_{i}}=

\displaystyle-m_{q_{i}}^{0}T\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\operatorname{tr}\,G_{q_{i}\bar{q}_{i}}(q)\,,

(226)

up to a renormalization term, where no summation for the index
i
is assumed, and
m_{q_{i}}^{0}
is the current quark mass. Obviously, the light quark condensate is given by
\Delta_{l}=\Delta_{u}=\Delta_{d}
with
m_{l}^{0}=m_{u}^{0}=m_{d}^{0}
. The renormalized condensate as follows

\displaystyle\Delta_{q_{i},R}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal N}_{R}}\left[\Delta_{q_{i}}(T,\mu_{q})-\Delta_{q_{i}}(0,0)\right]\,,

(227)

renders the vacuum part of the chiral condensate in
Equation

226
to be subtracted, where the dimensionless
\Delta_{q_{i},R}
is normalized by a constant
{\cal N}_{R}
, e.g.,
{\cal N}_{R}\sim f_{\pi}^{4}
. In the present fRG approach to QCD, the light quark condensate is given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m_{l}^{0}\frac{\partial\Omega[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}};T,\mu_{q}]}{\partial{m_{l}^{0}}}=\frac{1}{2}c_{\sigma}\frac{\partial\Omega[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}};T,\mu_{q}]}{\partial{c_{\sigma}}}\,,

(228)

where
\Omega
is the thermodynamic potential with the field
\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}}
being on is equation of motion. From
Equation

50
, one is led to

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}c_{\sigma}\,\sigma_{\mathrm{EoM}}(T,\mu_{q})\,,

(229)

and

\displaystyle\Delta_{l,R}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{\sigma}}{{2\,\cal N}_{R}}\Big[\sigma_{\mathrm{EoM}}(T,\mu_{q})-\sigma_{\mathrm{EoM}}(0,0)\Big]\,.

(230)

The reduced condensate
\Delta_{l,s}
is defined as the weighted difference between the light and strange quark condensates as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta_{l,s}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{\cal N}_{l,s}}\left[\Delta_{l}(T,\mu_{q})-\left(\frac{m_{l}^{0}}{m_{s}^{0}}\right)^{2}\Delta_{s}(T,\mu_{q})\right]\,,

(231)

which is usually normalized with its value in the vacuum, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Delta_{l,s}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{l}(T,\mu_{q})-\left(\frac{m_{l}^{0}}{m_{s}^{0}}\right)^{2}\Delta_{s}(T,\mu_{q})}{\Delta_{l}(0,0)-\left(\frac{m_{l}^{0}}{m_{s}^{0}}\right)^{2}\Delta_{s}(0,0)}\,.

(232)

Similar with
Equation

228
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\Delta_{s}=

\displaystyle m_{s}^{0}\frac{\partial\Omega[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}};T,\mu_{q}]}{\partial{m_{s}^{0}}}=c_{\sigma_{s}}\frac{\partial\Omega[\Phi_{\mathrm{EoM}};T,\mu_{q}]}{\partial{c_{\sigma_{s}}}}\,,

(233)

and thus

\displaystyle\Delta_{s}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}c_{\sigma_{s}}\,\sigma_{s,\textrm{EoM}}(T,\mu_{q})\,.

(234)

Finally, one is led to

\displaystyle\Delta_{l,s}(T,\mu_{q})=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\sigma-\sqrt{2}\frac{c_{\sigma}}{c_{\sigma_{s}}}\sigma_{s}\right)_{T,\mu_{q}}}{\left(\sigma-\sqrt{2}\frac{c_{\sigma}}{c_{\sigma_{s}}}\sigma_{s}\right)_{0,0}}\,,

(235)

where one has used

\displaystyle\frac{m_{l}^{0}}{m^{0}_{s}}=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\sigma}}{c_{\sigma_{s}}}\,,

(236)

In the left panel
Figure

46
, the renormalized light quark condensate is shown as a function of the temperature with several different values of baryon chemical potential. In the case of vanishing baryon chemical potential, the fRG result is compared with the continuum extrapolated result of lattice QCD
Borsanyi et al. 2010a
, and excellent agreement is found. In the right panel of
Figure

46
, the derivative of
\Delta_{l,R}
with respect to the temperature, i.e., the thermal susceptibility of the renormalized light quark condensate, are shown. The peak position of the thermal susceptibility can be used to define the pseudocritcal temperature, which is found to be
T_{c}=156
MeV for
\mu_{B}=0
, in good agreement with the lattice result. In
Figure

47
one shows the reduced condensate as a function of the temperature with
\mu_{B}=0
, in comparison to the lattice simulations. One finds that for the physical current quark mass ratio, i.e.,
m_{s}^{0}/m_{l}^{0}=c_{\sigma_{s}}/c_{\sigma}\approx 27

Aoki et al. 2014
, the fRG results are in quantitative agreement with the lattice ones.

Curvature
\kappa
of the phase boundary (second line) and location of CEP (third line) for
