Figure 48:
Phase diagram of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD in the plane of the temperature and the baryon chemical potential. The fRG results
Fu et al. 2020a
are compared with those from Dyson-Schwinger Equations
Gao and Pawlowski 2021a
;
Gunkel and Fischer 2021
, lattice QCD
Bellwied et al. 2015
;
Bazavov et al. 2019
. The hatched red area denotes the region of inhomogeneous instability for the chiral condensate found in the calculations of fRG. Some freeze-out data are also shown in the phase diagram:
Adamczyk et al. 2017
(STAR),
Alba et al. 2014
(Alba
et al.
),
Andronic et al. 2018
(Andronic
et al.
),
Becattini et al. 2017
(Becattini
et al.
),
Vovchenko et al. 2016
(Vovchenko
et al.
), and
Sagun et al. 2018
(Sagun
et al.
).

The QCD phase diagram in the plane of
T
and
\mu_{B}
is shown in
Figure

48
. The first-principle fRG results in
Fu et al. 2020a
are in comparison to state-of-the-art calculations of other functional approach, e.g., the Dyson-Schwinger Equations (DSE)
Gao and Pawlowski 2021a
;
Gunkel and Fischer 2021
, and lattice QCD
Bellwied et al. 2015
;
Bazavov et al. 2019
. The phase boundary in the regime of continuous crossover at small or medium
\mu_{B}
shows the dependence of the pseudocritical temperature on the value of the baryon chemical potential. In the calculations of fRG, this pseudocritical temperature is determined by the thermal susceptibility of the renormalized light quark chiral condensate,
\partial\Delta_{l,R}/\partial T
, as discussed in
Section

IV.5
. Expanding the pseudocritical temperature around
\mu_{B}=0
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\frac{T_{c}(\mu_{B})}{T_{c}}

\displaystyle=1-\kappa\left(\frac{\mu_{B}}{T_{c}}\right)^{2}+\lambda\left(\frac{\mu_{B}}{T_{c}}\right)^{4}+\cdots\,,

(237)

with
T_{c}=T_{c}(\mu_{B}=0)
, where the quadratic expansion coefficient
\kappa
is usually called as the curvature of the phase boundary. It is a sensitive measure for the QCD dynamics at finite temperature and densities. Therefore, it provides a benchmark test for functional approaches to make a comparison of the curvature at small baryon chemical potential with lattice simulations. The curvature values of the phase boundary obtained from fRG, DSE, and lattice QCD are summarized in the second line of
Table

3
. It is found recent results of the curvature from state-of-the-art functional approaches, e.g., fRG
Fu et al. 2020a
, DSE
Gao and Pawlowski 2021a
;
Gunkel and Fischer 2021
, have already been comparable to the lattice results. By contrast, those obtained from relatively former calculations of functional methods, e.g.,
Fischer et al. 2014b
;
Gao et al. 2016
;
Fischer 2019
, are significantly larger than the values of the curvature obtained from lattice simulations.

Besides the curvature of the phase boundary, another key ingredient of the QCD phase structure is the critical end point (CEP) of the first-order phase transition line at large baryon chemical potential, which is currently searched for with lots of efforts in experiments
Adamczyk et al. 2014a
;
Adamczyk et al. 2014b
;
Luo 2015
;
Luo and Xu 2017
;
Adamczyk et al. 2018
;
Adam et al. 2019
;
Adam et al. 2020
;
Abdallah et al. 2021a
. Lattice simulations, however, are restricted in the region of
\mu_{B}/T\lesssim 2\sim 3
because of the sign problem at finite chemical potentials, and in this region no signal of CEP is observed
Karsch 2019
. Passing lattice benchmark tests at small baryon chemical potentials, functional approaches are able to provide relatively reliable estimates for the location of the CEP. The recent results of CEP after benchmark testing are presented in the third line of
Table

3
, and also depicted in the phase diagram in
Figure

48
. Remarkably, estimates of CEP from fRG and DSE converge in a rather small region at baryon chemical potentials of about 600 MeV. Note that results of
Gunkel and Fischer 2021
in
Table

3
are obtained without the dynamics of sigma and pion, and the relevant results are
\kappa=0.0167
and
(T,{\mu_{B}})_{{}_{\tiny{\mathrm{CEP}}}}=(117,600)
MeV when they are included, and see also, e.g.,
Fischer and Luecker 2013
;
Fischer et al. 2014b
;
Fischer 2019
for related discussions. It should be reminded that errors of functional approaches increase significantly when
\mu_{B}/T\gtrsim 4
, for a detailed discussion see
Fu et al. 2020a
;
Gao and Pawlowski 2020
, and thus one arrives at a more reasonable estimation for the location of CEP as
450\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim{\mu_{B}}_{\tiny{\mathrm{CEP}}}\lesssim 650\,\mathrm{MeV}
.

IV.6.1
Region of inhomogeneous instability at large baryon chemical potential

Figure 49:
Left panel: Mesonic wave function renormalization at vanishing external momenta
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
as a function of the temperature with several different values of the baryon chemical potential, obtained in the fRG approach to
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD. Right panel:
\big|1/Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)\big|
for the data in the left panel. The spikes are used to locate the region of negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
. The plots are adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

Figure 50:
Phase diagram of
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD obtained in the fRG approach to QCD in comparison to freeze-out data. See also the caption of
Figure

48
. The blue area denotes the region of negative mesonic wave function renormalization at vanishing external momenta
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
, which is an indicator for the inhomogeneous instability. The red hatched area stands for the regime with negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
and also sizable chiral condensate. The plot is adopted from
Fu et al. 2020a
.

At large baryon chemical potentials, it is found within the fRG approach to QCD, that the mesonic wave function renormalization in
Equation

192
and
Equation

193
develops negative values at small momenta
Fu et al. 2020a
. As shown in
Figure

49
the mesonic wave function renormalization at vanishing external momenta
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
is depicted as a function of the temperature with several different values of
\mu_{B}
. One can see a negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
begins to appear for a temperature region when
\mu_{B}\gtrsim 420
MeV. The negative regime is clearly shown in the right plot of
\big|1/Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)\big|
between the two spikes, and it widens with the increase of the baryon chemical potential. From the two-point correlation functions of mesons as shown in Eqs. (
192
) and (
193
), the negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
implies that, for the dispersion relations of mesons, there is a minimum at a finite
\bm{p}^{2}\neq 0
. This nontrivial behavior that the minimum of dispersion is located at a finite momentum is indicative of an inhomogeneous instability, for instance, the formation of a spatially modulated chiral condensate. However, it should be reminded that a negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
is not bound to the formation of an inhomogeneous phase, it can also serve as an precursor for the inhomogeneous phase. See, e.g.,
Schon and Thies 2000
;
Thies 2006
;
Anglani et al. 2014
;
Carignano et al. 2014
;
Buballa and Carignano 2015
;
Braun et al. 2016b
;
Roscher et al. 2015
;
Yokota et al. 2017
;
Tripolt et al. 2018
;
Pisarski et al. 2019
;
Pisarski and Rennecke 2021
;
Rennecke and Pisarski 2021
for more related discussions. Most notably, very recently consequence of the inhomogeneous instability indicated by the negative wave function renormalization on the phenomenology of heavy-ion collisions has been studied. It is found that this inhomogeneous instability would result in a moat regime in both the particle
p_{T}
spectrum and the two-particle correlation, where the peaks are located at nonzero momenta
Pisarski and Rennecke 2021
;
Rennecke and Pisarski 2021
. The region of negative
Z_{\phi,k=0}(0)
is shown in
Figure

50
by the blue area, while the red hatched area shows where this region overlaps with a sizable chiral condensate.

IV.7
Magnetic equation of state

Figure 51:
Susceptibility for the reduced condensate obtained in the fRG approach to
N_{f}=2+1
flavor QCD (fQCD) as a function of the temperature, in comparison to the lattice results in
Ding et al. 2019a
;
Ding et al. 2019b
. The plot is adopted from
Braun et al. 2020a
.

Figure 52:
Pseudocritical temperature as a function of the pion mass obtained in fRG (fQCD) in comparison to the lattice results
Ding et al. 2019a
. The critical temperature in the chiral limit
T_{\mathrm{c}}
has been obtained from an extrapolation of the pseudocritical temperature
T_{\mathrm{pc}}
to
m_{\pi}\to 0
. The plot is adopted from
Braun et al. 2020a
.

From
Equation

228
,
Equation

230
, and
Equation

231
, it is convenient to define the corresponding susceptibilities for the light quark condensate, the renormalized light quark condensate, and the reduced condensate, i.e.,

\displaystyle\chi_{M}^{(i)}(T)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{l}^{0}}\left(\frac{\Delta_{i}(T)}{m_{l}^{0}}\right)\,,

(238)

with
(i)=(l),\,(l,R),\,(l,s)

Braun et al. 2020a
. Similar with the difference between the light quark condensate and the renormalized light quark condensate, the susceptibilities for them differ by the vacuum contribution. Among these three susceptibilities, the reduce susceptibility for the reduced condensate defined in fRG and lattice QCD, e.g.,
Ding et al. 2019a
, matches better.

In
Figure

51
the susceptibility obtained from the reduced condensate as a function of the temperature for different pion masses in the fRG approach
Braun et al. 2020a
, is compared with the lattice results
Ding et al. 2019a
. One can see that the fRG results are in good agreement with the lattice ones for pion masses
m_{\pi}\gtrsim 100
MeV. There are some slight deviations from the two approaches for small pion masses. From the dependence of the reduced susceptibility on the temperature for a fixed pion mass in
Figure

51
, one is able to define the pseudocritical temperature for the chiral crossover at such value of pion mass, via the peak position of the curves, denoted by
T_{\mathrm{pc}}
.

In
Figure

52
one shows the dependence of the pseudocritical temperature on the pion mass, obtained both from the fRG approach and the lattice QCD. The values of pseudocritical temperature at finite pion masses are also extrapolated to the chiral limit, i.e.,
m_{\pi}\to 0
, which leaves us with the critical temperature
T_{\mathrm{c}}
in the chiral limit. One obtains
T_{c}^{\mathrm{fRG}}\approx 142
MeV from the fRG approach
Braun et al. 2020a
, and
T_{c}^{\mathrm{lattice}}=132_{-6}^{+3}
MeV from the lattice QCD
Ding et al. 2019a
, that is, the critical temperature obtained in fRG is a bit larger than the lattice result. Recently, the critical temperature in the chiral limit from DSE is found to be
T_{c}^{\mathrm{DSE}}\approx 141
MeV
Gao and Pawlowski 2021b
.

From the general scaling hypothesis, see e.g.,
Ma 2000
, in the critical regime the pseudocritical temperature scales with the pion mass as

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{pc}}(m_{\pi})\approx T_{\mathrm{c}}+c\,m_{\pi}^{p}\,,

(239)

where
c
is a non-universal coefficient, while the exponent
p
is related to the critical exponents
\beta
and
\delta
through
p=2/(\beta\delta)

Braun et al. 2020a
. Inputting the values of
\beta
and
\delta
for the 3-
d

O(4)
universality class
Kanaya and Kaya 1995
;
Guida and Zinn-Justin 1998
;
Hasenbusch 2001
, one arrives at
p\approx 1.08
. On the contrary, fitting of the fRG data in
Figure

52
leads to
p\approx 0.91_{-0.03}^{+0.03}

Braun et al. 2020a
. This discrepancy indicates that the pion masses under investigation in
Braun et al. 2020a
, i.e.,
m_{\pi}\gtrsim 30
MeV are beyond the critical regime. In fact, the critical regime is found to be extremely small,
m_{\pi}\lesssim 1
MeV, in fRG studies of LEFTs, and see, e.g.,
Schaefer and Wambach 2007
;
Braun and Klein 2008
;
Braun et al. 2011a
;
Chen et al. 2021
for a more detailed discussion.

V
Real-time fRG

In this section we would like to give a brief introduction about the real-time fRG, based on a combination of the fRG approach and the formalism of Schwinger-Keldysh path integral, where the flow equations are formulated on the closed time path. The Schwinger-Keldysh path integral is devised to study real-time quantum dynamics
Schwinger 1961
;
Keldysh 1964
, which has been proved be very powerful to cope with problems of both equilibrium and nonequilibrium many-body systems, see, e.g.,
Chou et al. 1985
;
Blaizot and Iancu 2002
;
Berges 2004
;
Sieberer et al. 2016
for relevant reviews.

Within the fRG approach on the closed time path, nonthermal fixed points of the
O(N)
scalar theory are investigated
Berges and Hoffmeister 2009
;
Berges and Mesterhazy 2012
. The transition from unitary to dissipative dynamics is studied
Mesterházy et al. 2015
. Spectral functions in a scalar field theory with
d
=0+1 dimensions are computed within the real-time fRG approach
Huelsmann et al. 2020
. Moreover, it has also been employed to study the nonequilibrium transport, dynamical critical behavior, etc. in open quantum systems
Jakobs et al. 2007
;
Sieberer et al. 2013
, see also
Sieberer et al. 2016
;
Dupuis et al. 2021
for related reviews. Recently, the real-time fRG is compared with other real-time methods
Roth et al. 2021
.

Furthermore, it should be mentioned that another conceptually new fRG with the Keldysh functional integral is put forward in
Gasenzer and Pawlowski 2008
;
Gasenzer et al. 2010
;
Corell et al. 2019
;
Heller and Pawlowski 2021
, and the regulation of the RG scale there is replaced with that of the time, which is also called as the temporal renormalization group. Noteworthily, recently the Källén-Lehmann spectral representation of correlation functions has been used in the approach of DSE, to study the spectral functions in the
\phi^{4}
-theory and Yang-Mills theory
Horak et al. 2020
;
Horak et al. 2021
;
Horak et al. 2022b
. The spectral functions provide important informations on the time-like properties of correlation functions, see, e.g.,
Haas et al. 2014
;
Christiansen et al. 2015
;
Cyrol et al. 2018c
;
Binosi and Tripolt 2020
. Besides the real-time fRG, another commonly adopted approach is the analytically continued functional renormalization group, where the Euclidean flow equations are analytically continued into the Minkowski spacetime on the level of analytic expressions with some specific truncations, and see, e.g.,
Tripolt et al. 2014a
;
Tripolt et al. 2014c
;
Pawlowski and Strodthoff 2015
;
Jung et al. 2017
;
Jung et al. 2021
for more details.

In this section we would like to discuss the fRG formulated on the Keldysh path at the example of the
O(N)
scalar theory
Tan et al. 2022
.

V.1
fRG with the Keldysh functional integral

On the closed time path the classical action in
Equation

1
for a closed system reads

\displaystyle S[\hat{\Phi}]

\displaystyle=\int_{x}\Big(\mathcal{L}[\hat{\Phi}_{+}]-\mathcal{L}[\hat{\Phi}_{-}]\Big)\,,

(240)

with
\int_{x}\equiv\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt\int d^{3}x
, where the subscripts
\pm
denote variables on the forward and backward time branches, respectively
Chou et al. 1985
. Now the Keldysh generating functional reads

\displaystyle Z[J_{+},J_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\big(\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{+}\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{-}\big)\exp\Big\{i\Big(S[\hat{\Phi}]+\big(J_{+}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,+}-J_{-}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,-}\big)\Big)\Big\}\,.

(241)

It is more convenient to adopt the physical representation in terms of the “classical” and “quantum” variables, denoted by subscripts
c
and
q
, respectively, which are related to variables on the two time branches by a Keldysh rotation, that is,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\hat{\Phi}_{i,+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}+\hat{\Phi}_{i,q}),\\[12.91663pt]
\hat{\Phi}_{i,-}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}-\hat{\Phi}_{i,q}),\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}J_{+}^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(J_{c}^{i}+J_{q}^{i}),\\[12.91663pt]
J_{-}^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(J_{c}^{i}-J_{q}^{i}),\end{array}\right.

Then,
Equation

241
reads

\displaystyle Z[J_{c},J_{q}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\big(\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{c}\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{q}\big)\exp\Big\{i\Big(S[\hat{\Phi}]+\big(J_{q}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}+J_{c}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,q}\big)\Big)\Big\}\,.

(246)

The bilinear regulator term in
Equation

2
can be chosen as

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\Phi}_{i,c},\hat{\Phi}_{i,q})\begin{pmatrix}0&R_{k}^{ij}\\
(R_{k}^{ij})^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\Phi}_{j,c}\\
\hat{\Phi}_{j,q}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big(\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}R_{k}^{ij}\hat{\Phi}_{j,q}+\hat{\Phi}_{i,q}(R_{k}^{ij})^{*}\hat{\Phi}_{j,c}\Big)\,.

(247)

Then the RG scale
k
-dependent generating function
Equation

1
with the Keldysh functional integral reads

\displaystyle Z_{k}[J_{c},J_{q}]=

\displaystyle\int\big(\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{c}\mathcal{D}\hat{\Phi}_{q}\big)\exp\Big\{i\Big(S[\hat{\Phi}]+\Delta S_{k}[\hat{\Phi}]

\displaystyle+(J_{q}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}+J_{c}^{i}\hat{\Phi}_{i,q})\Big)\Big\}\,.

(248)

The Schwinger functional in
Equation

14
reads

\displaystyle W_{k}[J_{c},J_{q}]

\displaystyle=-i\ln Z_{k}[J_{c},J_{q}]\,.

(249)

Combining the indices
c,\,q
and
i
for other degrees of freedom into one single label, say
a
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\{\hat{\Phi}_{a}\}

\displaystyle=\big\{\{\hat{\Phi}_{i,c}\},\{\hat{\Phi}_{i,q}\}\big\}\,,

(250)

\displaystyle\{J^{a}\}

\displaystyle=\big\{\{J_{q}^{i}\},\{J_{c}^{i}\}\big\}\,.

(251)

one is able to simplify notations significantly, for instance, the regulator term in
Equation

247
now reading

\displaystyle\Delta S_{k}[\varphi]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hat{\Phi}_{a}R_{k}^{ab}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\,,

(252)

with

\displaystyle R_{k}^{ab}\equiv

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&R_{k}^{ij}\\
(R_{k}^{ij})^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(253)

In the same way, the expectation value of the field reads

\displaystyle\Phi_{a}

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\rangle=\frac{\delta W_{k}[J]}{\delta J^{a}}\,,

(254)

and the propagator is given by

\displaystyle G_{k,ab}\equiv

\displaystyle-i\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle_{c}=-i\big[\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle-\langle\hat{\Phi}_{a}\rangle\langle\hat{\Phi}_{b}\rangle\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta^{2}W_{k}[J]}{\delta J^{a}J^{b}}\,.

(255)

The effective action is obtained from the Schwinger functional upon a Legendre transformation, viz.,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=W_{k}[J]-\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]-J^{a}\Phi_{a}\,.

(256)

Similar with
Equation

21
and
Equation

22
, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}[\Phi])}{\delta\Phi_{a}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{a}_{\hskip 4.26773ptb}J^{b}\,,

(257)

and

\displaystyle G_{k,ab}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{c}_{\hskip 4.26773pta}\Big[\big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\big)^{-1}\Big]_{cb}\,.

(258)

with

\displaystyle\Big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\Big)^{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta^{2}(\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]+\Delta S_{k}[\Phi])}{\delta\Phi_{a}\delta\Phi_{b}}\,.

(259)

Then it is left to specify the flow equation of Schwinger functional with the Keldysh functional integral, that reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}W_{k}[J]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}iG_{k,ab}\partial_{\tau}R^{ab}_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{a}\big(\partial_{\tau}R^{ab}_{k}\big)\Phi_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\mathrm{STr}\Big[\big(\partial_{\tau}R^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\big)G_{k}\Big]+\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{a}\big(\partial_{\tau}R^{ab}_{k}\big)\Phi_{b}\,,

(260)

where the RG time is denoted by
\tau\equiv\ln(k/\Lambda)
, and
R^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}
stands for the transpose of the regulator only in the
c
-
q
space as shown in
Equation

253
. Finally, the flow equation of the effective action reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}W_{k}[J]-\partial_{\tau}\Delta S_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\mathrm{STr}\Big[\big(\partial_{\tau}R^{\mathrm{T}}_{k}\big)G_{k}\Big]\,.

(261)

V.2
Real-time
O(N)
scalar theory

Figure 53:
Diagrammatic representation of the three different propagators for the sigma and pion mesons. A line associated with two end points labelled with “
c,q
” denotes the retarded propagator, while that with
“
q,c
” denotes the advanced propagator. A line with an empty circle inserted in the middle is used to denote the Keldysh propagator, which results from
Equation

285
. The plot is adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

The Keldysh effective action in
Equation

256
for the
O(N)
scalar theory reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{k}[\phi_{c},\phi_{q}]=

\displaystyle\int_{x}\Big[Z_{\phi,k}(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{q})\cdot(\partial^{\mu}\phi_{c})-U_{k}(\phi_{c},\phi_{q})\Big]\,,

(262)

where the potential is given by

\displaystyle U_{k}(\phi_{c},\phi_{q})=

\displaystyle V_{k}(\rho_{+})-V_{k}(\rho_{-})\,,

(263)

with
\rho_{\pm}=\phi_{\pm}^{2}/2
. Here
\phi_{i,\pm}
and
\phi_{i,c/q}
(
i=0,1,...N-1
) denote the fields of
N
components on the closed time branches or in the physical representation, respectively, and their relations are given in
Section

V.1
. Note that in
Equation

262
a local potential approximation with a
k
-dependent wave function renormalization
Z_{\phi,k}
has been adopted, which is usually called the LPA’ approximation.

When the
O(N)
symmetry is broken into the
O(N-1)
one in the direction of component
i=0
, the expectation values of the fields read

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{i,c}=

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{c}\delta_{i0}\,,\qquad\bar{\phi}_{i,q}=0\,.

(264)

Then, the sigma and pion fields are give by

\displaystyle\sigma_{c}=

\displaystyle\phi_{0,c}-\bar{\phi}_{c}\,,\qquad\sigma_{q}=\phi_{0,q}\,,

(265)

and

\displaystyle\pi_{i,c}=

\displaystyle\phi_{i,c}\,,\qquad\pi_{i,q}=\phi_{i,q}\,,\qquad(i\neq 0)\,.

(266)

The sigma and pion masses read

\displaystyle m_{\sigma,k}^{2}\equiv

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})+2\bar{\rho}_{c}V^{(2)}_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})\,,

(267)

\displaystyle m_{\pi,k}^{2}\equiv

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})\,,

(268)

with
\bar{\rho}_{c}\equiv{\bar{\phi}_{c}}^{2}/4
.

The regulator in
Equation

253
in the case of the
O(N)
scalar theory reads

\displaystyle R_{k}(q)=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&R_{k}^{A}(q)\\[8.61108pt]
R_{k}^{R}(q)&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(269)

with

\displaystyle R_{k}^{R}(q)

\displaystyle=R_{k}^{A}(q)=\begin{pmatrix}R_{\sigma,k}(q)&0\\[8.61108pt]
0&R_{\pi,k}(q)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(270)

where one has

\displaystyle R_{\sigma,k}(q)=

\displaystyle R_{\phi,k}(q)\,,\qquad\big(R_{\pi,k}\big)_{ij}(q)=R_{\phi,k}(q)\delta_{ij}\,,

(271)

with

\displaystyle R_{\phi,k}(q)=

\displaystyle Z_{\phi,k}\Big(-{\bm{q}}^{2}r_{B}\big(\frac{{\bm{q}}^{2}}{k^{2}}\big)\Big)\,.

(272)

Here, the
3d
flat regulator is used.

The flow equation of effective action in
Equation

261
with the regulator in
Equation

269
can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\mathrm{STr}\Big[\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}\ln\big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]+R_{k}\big)\Big]\,,

(273)

with

\displaystyle\Big(\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}[\Phi]\Big)_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\Phi_{a}\delta\Phi_{b}}\,,

(274)

where the fluctuation matrix can be decomposed into a sum of the inverse propagator
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and the interaction
\mathcal{F}_{k}
as shown in
Equation

60
. In thermal equilibrium at a temperature
T
, the
\mathcal{P}_{k}
matrix reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathcal{P}^{A}_{k}\\[4.30554pt]
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{k}&\mathcal{P}^{K}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(275)

where the inverse retarded propagator is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{R}_{k}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\sigma,k}&0\\[8.61108pt]
0&\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\pi,k}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(276)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\sigma,k}=

\displaystyle Z_{\phi,k}\bigg[q_{0}^{2}-{\bm{q}}^{2}\Big(1+r_{B}\big(\frac{{\bm{q}}^{2}}{k^{2}}\big)\Big)\bigg]-m_{\sigma,k}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(q_{0})i\epsilon\,,

(277)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\pi,k}\big)_{ij}=

\displaystyle\Bigg\{Z_{\phi,k}\bigg[q_{0}^{2}-{\bm{q}}^{2}\Big(1+r_{B}\big(\frac{{\bm{q}}^{2}}{k^{2}}\big)\Big)\bigg]-m_{\pi,k}^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(q_{0})i\epsilon\Bigg\}\delta_{ij}\,.

(278)

The inverse advanced propagator is related to the retarded one through a complex conjugate, to wit,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{A}_{k}=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{R}_{k})^{*}\,.

(279)

The
qq
component of the inverse propagator in
Equation

275
, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}^{K}_{k}
, reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{K}_{k}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{P}^{K}_{\sigma,k}&0\\[8.61108pt]
0&\mathcal{P}^{K}_{\pi,k}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(280)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{K}_{\sigma,k}=

\displaystyle 2i\epsilon\,\mathrm{sgn}(q_{0})\coth\Big(\frac{q_{0}}{2T}\Big)\,,

(281)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{P}^{K}_{\pi,k}\big)_{ij}=

\displaystyle\bigg[2i\epsilon\,\mathrm{sgn}(q_{0})\coth\Big(\frac{q_{0}}{2T}\Big)\bigg]\delta_{ij}\,.

(282)

Then, one can obtain the propagator matrix as follows

\displaystyle G_{k}=

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{P}_{k}\big)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}G^{K}_{k}&G^{R}_{k}\\[4.30554pt]
G^{A}_{k}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(283)

with the retarded and advanced propagator being

\displaystyle G^{R}_{k}=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{R}_{k})^{-1}\,,\qquad G^{A}_{k}=(\mathcal{P}^{A}_{k})^{-1}\,,

(284)

and the correlation function or Keldysh propagator being

\displaystyle G^{K}_{k}=

\displaystyle-(\mathcal{P}^{R}_{k})^{-1}\mathcal{P}^{K}_{k}(\mathcal{P}^{A}_{k})^{-1}=-G^{R}_{k}\mathcal{P}^{K}_{k}G^{A}_{k}\,.

(285)

Finally, one can verify the fluctuation-dissipation relation in thermal equilibrium, as follows

\displaystyle G^{K}_{k}=

\displaystyle\big(G^{R}_{k}-G^{A}_{k}\big)\coth\Big(\frac{q_{0}}{2T}\Big)\,.

(286)

From
Equation

283
one arrives at the three different propagators which read

\displaystyle iG^{R}_{\phi,k}=

\displaystyle\langle T_{p}\phi_{c}(x)\phi_{q}(y)\rangle\,,\quad iG^{A}_{\phi,k}=\langle T_{p}\phi_{q}(x)\phi_{c}(y)\rangle\,,

(287)

\displaystyle iG^{K}_{\phi,k}=

\displaystyle\langle T_{p}\phi_{c}(x)\phi_{c}(y)\rangle=\big(iG^{R}_{\phi,k}\big)\big(i\mathcal{P}^{K}_{\phi,k}\big)\big(iG^{A}_{\phi,k}\big)\,,

(288)

where
T_{p}
denotes the time ordering from the positive to negative time branch in the Keldysh closed time path, and
Equation

288
follows directly from
Equation

285
. Diagrammatic representation of these propagators is shown in
Figure

53
.

Figure 54:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equation of the effective potential in the real-time
O(N)
scalar theory. The vertices are denoted by gray blobs, and their legs are distinguished for the “
q
” and “
c
” fields. the crossed circles stand for the regulator insertion. The plot is adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

Projecting the flow equation of the effective action in
Equation

273
onto a derivative with respect to the quantum sigma field
\sigma_{q}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left.\partial_{\tau}\left(\frac{i\delta\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\sigma_{q}}\right)\right|_{\Phi=0}\,,

(289)

with
\Phi=(\sigma_{c},\{\pi_{i,c}\},\sigma_{q},\{\pi_{i,q}\})
, one is led to

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}V^{\prime}_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}\Big[\frac{\partial m_{\sigma,k}^{2}}{\partial\bar{\rho}_{c}}G^{K}_{\sigma,k}(q)+\frac{\partial m_{\pi,k}^{2}}{\partial\bar{\rho}_{c}}\big(G^{K}_{\pi,k}\big)_{ii}(q)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\rho}_{c}}\bigg\{-\frac{i}{4}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\big(\partial_{\tau}R_{\phi,k}(q)\big)\Big[G^{K}_{\sigma,k}(q)

\displaystyle+\big(G^{K}_{\pi,k}\big)_{ii}(q)\Big]\bigg\}\,,

(290)

which is diagrammatically shown in
Figure

54
. Integrating both sides of
Equation

290
over
\bar{\rho}_{c}
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}V_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{4}\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\big(\partial_{\tau}R_{\phi,k}(q)\big)\Big[G^{K}_{\sigma,k}(q)+\big(G^{K}_{\pi,k}\big)_{ii}(q)\Big]\,,

(291)

up to a term independent of
\bar{\rho}_{c}
. Inserting
Equation

277
and
Equation

278
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}V_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{c})=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{4}}{4\pi^{2}}\bigg[l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\sigma,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle+\big(N-1\big)l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)\bigg]\,,

(292)

with the threshold function given by

\displaystyle l^{(B,4)}_{0}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k},\eta_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{\phi,k}}{5}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k}}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+n_{B}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k};T)\right)\,,

(293)

and the renormalized dimensionless meson masses reading

\displaystyle\tilde{m}^{2}_{\sigma,k}=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}_{\sigma,k}}{k^{2}Z_{\phi,k}}\,,\qquad\tilde{m}^{2}_{\pi,k}=\frac{m^{2}_{\pi,k}}{k^{2}Z_{\phi,k}}\,,

(294)

where the bosonic distribution function reads

\displaystyle n_{B}(\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k};T)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\exp\bigg\{\frac{k}{T}\sqrt{1+\tilde{m}^{2}_{\phi,k}}\bigg\}-1}\,.

(295)

Note that
Equation

292
is just the flow equation of the effective potential in the LPA’ approximation obtained in the conventional Euclidean formalism of fRG.

V.3
Flows of the two- and four-point correlation functions

Figure 55:
Diagrammatic representation of the flow equation of the four-point vertex in the symmetric phase. The plot is adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

The 1PI
n
-point correlation function or vertex in the Euclidean field theory is given in
Equation

62
, and the counterpart in the Keldysh field theory reads

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{k,\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\Phi_{a_{n}}}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\left.\left(\frac{i\delta^{n}\Gamma_{k}[\Phi]}{\delta\Phi_{a_{1}}\cdots\delta\Phi_{a_{n}}}\right)\right|_{\Phi=\langle\Phi\rangle}\,.

(296)

Note that a label “
c
” or “
q
” is associated with an external leg of the vertex to distinguish the classical or quantum field, as shown in
Figure

54
. In the following we consider the symmetric phase with
\bar{\phi}_{c}=0
in
Equation

264
. In this case the pion and sigma fields are degenerate, and they are collectively denoted by
\phi_{i}
(
i=0,1,...N-1
). The diagrammatic representation of the four-point vertex
i\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}\phi_{c}\phi_{c}}^{(4)}
reads

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{k,\phi_{i,q}\phi_{j,c}\phi_{k,c}\phi_{l,c}}^{(4)}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},p_{l})

\displaystyle\equiv\parbox[c]{51.7479pt}{\includegraphics[width]{Gam4-piq1pic3-labels}}\,,

(297)

whose flow equation is given in
Figure

55
. According to the generic interchange symmetry of the external legs, the four-point vertex can be parameterized as

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{k,\phi_{i,q}\phi_{j,c}\phi_{k,c}\phi_{l,c}}^{(4)}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},p_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{3}\Big[\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},p_{l})\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}+\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{i},p_{k},p_{l},p_{j})\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}

\displaystyle+\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{i},p_{l},p_{j},p_{k})\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\Big]\,,

(298)

where an effective four-point coupling
\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}
is introduced. In the following, one adopts the truncation that the momentum dependence of the four-point vertex on the r.h.s. of the flow equation in
Figure

55
is neglected, and the four-point vertex there is identified as

\displaystyle\lambda_{4\pi,k}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(0)\,.

(299)

Then, from the flow of the four-point vertex in
Figure

55
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{i},p_{j},p_{k},p_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{2}}{3}\Big[(N+4)\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}I_{k}(-p_{i}-p_{l})+2\,\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}I_{k}(-p_{i}-p_{k})

\displaystyle+2\,\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}I_{k}(-p_{i}-p_{j})\Big]\,,

(300)

with

\displaystyle I_{k}(p)

\displaystyle\equiv i\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}G^{K}_{\pi,k}(q)G^{A}_{\pi,k}(q-p)\,.

(301)

Note that the wave function renormalization
Z_{\phi,k}=1
is adopted to simplify the calculation of
\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}I_{k}(p)
in
Equation

300
. The explicit expression of
I_{k}(p)
can be found in
Tan et al. 2022
.

With the four-point vertex in
Equation

298
one is able to obtain the self-energy as follows

\displaystyle-i\Sigma_{k,ij}(p)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\parbox[c]{51.7479pt}{\includegraphics[width]{Gam2-piqpic-piloop-labels}}\,,

(302)

which reads

\displaystyle-i\Sigma_{k,ij}(p)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}(-\frac{i}{6})(N+2)

\displaystyle\times\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}iG^{K}_{\pi,k}(q)\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|,q_{0},|\bm{q}|,\cos\theta)\,.

(303)

Here, the function
\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|,q_{0},|\bm{q}|,\cos\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N+2}\Big[N\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(-p,-q,q,p)+\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(-p,p,-q,q)

\displaystyle+\lambda_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(-p,q,p,-q)\Big]\,,

(304)

with
\theta
being the angle between the two momenta
\bm{p}
and
\bm{q}
. The inverse retarded propagator, i.e., the two-point correlation function with one
q
field and one
c
field, is given by

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{k,\phi_{i,q}\phi_{j,c}}^{(2)}(p)\equiv

\displaystyle i\frac{\delta^{2}\Gamma_{k}[\phi]}{\delta\phi_{i,q}(p)\delta\phi_{j,c}(-p)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\delta_{ij}\Big(Z_{\phi,k}(p^{2})p^{2}-m_{\pi,k}^{2}\Big)\,,

(305)

and its flow equation reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p)=-\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}\Sigma_{k}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-\frac{i}{6})(N+2)\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{\partial}_{\tau}\Big(G^{K}_{\pi,k}(q)\Big)

\displaystyle\times\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|,q_{0},|\bm{q}|,\cos\theta)\,.

(306)

Substituting the Keldysh propagator in
Equation

285
, one arrives at

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)\mathrm{I}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)+\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)\mathrm{II}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\,.

(307)

The first part on the r.h.s. of
Equation

307
reads

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)\mathrm{I}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24}\frac{(N+2)}{(2\pi)^{2}}\bigg[-\frac{\coth\Big(\frac{E_{\pi,k}(k)}{2T}\Big)}{\big(E_{\pi,k}(k)\big)^{3}}-\frac{\mathrm{csch}^{2}\Big(\frac{E_{\pi,k}(k)}{2T}\Big)}{2T\big(E_{\pi,k}(k)\big)^{2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\times(2k^{2})\int_{0}^{k}d|\bm{q}||\bm{q}|^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}d\cos\theta\Big[\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}\big|_{q_{0}=E_{\pi,k}(k)}

\displaystyle+\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}\big|_{q_{0}=-E_{\pi,k}(k)}\Big]\,,

(308)

and the second part is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)\mathrm{II}}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24}\frac{(N+2)}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\coth\Big(\frac{E_{\pi,k}(k)}{2T}\Big)}{\big(E_{\pi,k}(k)\big)^{2}}(2k^{2})\int_{0}^{k}d|\bm{q}||\bm{q}|^{2}

\displaystyle\times\int_{-1}^{1}d\cos\theta\Big[\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}\big|_{q_{0}=E_{\pi,k}(k)}

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{0}}\bar{\lambda}_{4\pi,k}^{\mathrm{eff}}\big|_{q_{0}=-E_{\pi,k}(k)}\Big]\,.

(309)

with

\displaystyle E_{\pi,k}(k)=

\displaystyle\Big(k^{2}+m^{2}_{\pi,k}\Big)^{1/2}\,,

(310)

where
m_{\pi,k}^{2}
can be extracted from the two-point correlation function at vanishing momentum, viz.,

\displaystyle m_{\pi,k}^{2}=

\displaystyle-\Gamma_{k,\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(0)\,.

(311)

Note that the second part in
Equation

309
is negligible if the momentum dependence of the vertex is mild.

V.4
Spectral functions and dynamical critical exponent

Figure 56:
Spectral function
\rho(p_{0},|\bm{p}|=0)
as a function of
p_{0}
with several small (left panel, close to the critical temperature
T_{c}=20.4
MeV) and large (right panel) values of temperature obtained in the real-time
O(4)
scalar theory within the fRG approach. The plots are adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

Figure 57:
3D plots of the spectral function as a function of
p_{0}
and
|\bm{p}|
at temperature
T=54
MeV (left panel) and
145
MeV (right panel), obtained in the real-time
O(4)
scalar theory within the fRG approach. The plots are adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

Figure 58:
Double logarithm plot of the relaxation rate
\omega(|\bm{p}|)
in
Equation

321
as a function of the spacial momentum with
p_{0}=0
at the critical temperature
T_{c}=20.4
MeV, obtained in the real-time 3
d

O(4)
scalar theory within the fRG approach. The plot is adopted from
Tan et al. 2022
.

The retarded propagator in the Källén-Lehmann spectral representation is related to the spectral function
\rho
via a relation as follows

\displaystyle G_{R}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{dp_{0}^{\prime}}{2\pi}\frac{\rho(p_{0}^{\prime},|\bm{p}|)}{p_{0}^{\prime}-(p_{0}+i\epsilon)}\,.

(312)

Thus, the spectral function is proportional to the imaginary part of the retarded propagator, i.e.,

\displaystyle\rho(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle-2\Im\,G_{R}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\,.

(313)

Notice that here the IR limit
k\to 0
is tacitly assumed. The retarded propagator is just the inverse of the two-point correlation function, to wit,

\displaystyle G_{R}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle\left[\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\right]^{-1}\,.

(314)

Consequently, the spectral function can be expressed in terms of the real and imaginary parts of the two-point correlation function, that is,

\displaystyle\rho(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle\frac{2\Im\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)}{\left[\Re\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\right]^{2}+\left[\Im\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\right]^{2}}\,.

(315)

Evidently, one has

\displaystyle\rho(-p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle-\rho(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\,.

(316)

In
Figure

56
, the spectral function
\rho(p_{0},|\bm{p}|=0)
is shown as a function of
p_{0}
with different values of the temperature. In the left panel, the temperatures are above but close to the critical temperature
T_{c}=20.4
MeV for the phase transition, where the symmetry is broken from
O(N)
to
O(N-1)
when
T<T_{c}
, while in the right panel, the temperatures are far larger than
T_{c}
. One can see that when temperature is large, one has a negative spectral function in the regime of small
p_{0}
, and there is a minus peak structure around the pole mass. It is found that the negative spectral function results from the contributions of the Landau damping, and see
Tan et al. 2022
for a more detailed discussion. Nonetheless, when the temperature is decreased below about 60 MeV, the Landau damping is dominated by the process of creation and annihilation of particles, and the spectral function is positive. Moreover, when the temperature is more and more close to the critical temperature, the peak structure on the spectral function becomes more and more wider, and finally disappears at the critical temperature. The 3D plots of the spectral function as a function of
p_{0}
and
|\bm{p}|
are shown in
Figure

57
with two different values of the temperature.

We proceed to the discussion of the dynamical critical exponent, and begin with the kinetic coefficient
\Gamma(|\bm{p}|)
that is defined as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(|\bm{p}|)}=

\displaystyle-i\frac{\partial\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)}{\partial p_{0}}\bigg|_{p_{0}=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Im\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)}{\partial p_{0}}\bigg|_{p_{0}=0}\,,

(317)

where the parity properties of the real and imaginary parts of the two-point correlation function have been used, i.e.,

\displaystyle\Re\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(-p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle\Re\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\,,

(318)

\displaystyle\Im\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(-p_{0},|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle-\Im\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0},|\bm{p}|)\,.

(319)

The relaxation rate, or dissipative characteristic frequency, is given by

\displaystyle\omega(|\bm{p}|)=

\displaystyle\Gamma(|\bm{p}|)\left(-\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0}=0,|\bm{p}|)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma(|\bm{p}|)\Re\,\Gamma_{\phi_{q}\phi_{c}}^{(2)}(p_{0}=0,|\bm{p}|)\,.

(320)

When the momentum is larger than the correlation length
|\bm{p}|>\xi^{-1}
, with
\xi\sim m_{\phi}^{-1}
, the relaxation rate scales as

\displaystyle\omega(|\bm{p}|)\propto

\displaystyle|\bm{p}|^{z}\,,

(321)

through which one can extract the dynamical critical exponent
z

Hohenberg and Halperin 1977
. From
Figure

58
the value of the dynamical critical exponent in the real-time 3
d

O(4)
scalar theory is extracted, and one arrives at
z=2.02284(6)
, where the numerical error is shown in the bracket.

According to the standard classification for universalities of the critical dynamics
Hohenberg and Halperin 1977
, it is argued that the critical dynamics of the relativistic
O(4)
scalar theory belongs to the universality of Model G
Rajagopal and Wilczek 1993
, see also
Schlichting et al. 2020
, which leaves us with
z=3/2
in three dimensions. The dynamic critical exponent for the
O(4)
model is also calculated in real-time classical-statistical lattice simulations, and it is found that
z
is in favor of 2, but there is still a sizable numerical error
Schlichting et al. 2020
. The dynamic critical exponent in a relativistic
O(N)
vector model is also found to be close to 2
Mesterházy et al. 2015
. Similar result is found in a
O(3)
model in
Duclut and Delamotte 2017
. Moreover,
z=1.92(11)
is found for Model A in three spatial dimensions from real-time classical-statistical lattice simulations
Schweitzer et al. 2020
. In short, the dynamic critical exponent is far from clear and conclusive in comparison to the static critical exponent, and more studies of the critical dynamics from different approaches, including the real-time fRG, are necessary and desirable in the future.

VI
Conclusions

In this paper we present an overview on recent progress in studies of QCD at finite temperature and densities within the fRG approach. After a brief introduction of the formalism, the fRG approach is applied in low energy effective field theories (LEFTs) and the first-principle QCD. The mechanism of quark mass production and natural emergence of bound states is well illustrated within the fRG approach, and a set of self-consistent flow equations of different correlation functions provide the necessary resummations, and plays the same role as the quark gap equation and Bethe-Salpeter equation of bound states.

We present results for the QCD phase structure and the location of the critical end point (CEP), the QCD equation of state (EoS), the magnetic EoS, baryon number fluctuations confronted with recent experimental measurements, various critical exponents, etc. It is found that the non-monotonic dependence of the kurtosis of the net-baryon or net-proton (proxy for net-baryon in experiments) number distributions could arise from the increasingly sharp crossover with the decrease of the beam collision energy, which in turn indicates that the non-monotonicity observed in experiments is highly non-trivial. Furthermore, recent estimates of the location of the CEP from first-principle QCD calculations within fRG and Dyson-Schwinger Equations, which passes through lattice benchmark tests at small baryon chemical potentials, converge in a rather small region at baryon chemical potentials of about 600 MeV. But it should be reminded that errors of functional approaches increase significantly in the regime of
\mu_{B}/T\gtrsim 4
, and thus one arrives at a more reasonable estimation for the location of CEP as
450\,\mathrm{MeV}\lesssim{\mu_{B}}_{\tiny{\mathrm{CEP}}}\lesssim 650\,\mathrm{MeV}
. Moreover, a region of inhomogeneous instability indicated by a negative wave function renormalization is found with
\mu_{B}\gtrsim 420
MeV, and its consequence on the phenomenology of heavy-ion collisions has been investigated very recently
Pisarski and Rennecke 2021
;
Rennecke and Pisarski 2021
. It is found that this inhomogeneous instability would result in a moat regime in both the particle
p_{T}
spectrum and the two-particle correlation. By investigating the critical behavior and extracting critical exponents in the vicinity of the CEP, it is found that the size of the critical region is extremely small, quite smaller than
\sim 1
MeV.

In this review we also discuss the real-time fRG, in which the flow equations are formulated on the Schwinger-Keldysh closed time path. By organizing the effective action and the flow equations in terms of the “classical” and “quantum” fields, one is able to give a concise diagrammatic representation for the flow equations of propagators and vertices in the real-time fRG. The spectral functions of the
O(N)
scalar theory in the critical regime in the proximity of the critical temperature are obtained. The dynamical critical exponent in the
O(4)
scalar theory in
3+1
dimensions is found to be
z\simeq 2.023
.
